- The central `−1 ∈ Spin₁₀` acts on `S⁺` by `−1`, so there is no rank-sixteen half-spin vector bundle on `BSO₁₀`; the element `−1 ∈ SO₁₀` lifts to elements of order four acting by `±i`, which act trivially on `ℙS⁺` and on Grassmannians of `S⁺`.
- A generic pentagonal genus-seven curve has trivial automorphism group, while `[I_0/SO₁₀]` would give every point a `μ₂` stabilizer from the centre of `SO₁₀`; the comparison is with the effective quotient by `PSO₁₀`.
- At `n = 0` the model consists of seven-dimensional subspaces of the sixteen-dimensional half-spin space.

### 7.4 Descent of bundles and rational Chow transfer for the Mukai model

On the `Spin₁₀`-lift of §7.3 compute the characters of the central `μ₄` on `S⁺`, on each tautological line `L_i`, on the quotient `Q_n = S⁺/⊕L_i` and on the tautological subbundle `E_n ⊂ Q_n` of rank `7 − n`. Prove that pullback along the gerbe `[I_n/Spin₁₀] → M°_{7,n}` is an isomorphism on rational Chow groups, naturally after product with every finite-type stack stratified by quotient stacks, and that the CKgP descends along it; identify which character-zero combinations of these bundles descend, and compare their Chern classes with classes on `M°_{7,n}` compatibly with forgetting markings (CLP, Lemmas 5.13–5.17, pp. 20–22, read on the lift). Route: a gerbe banded by a finite group induces an isomorphism on rational Chow groups after every product; the map is proper, surjective and representable by DM stacks, so §5.2 applies; Chern classes of bundles of nonzero central character are compared through their tensor products with a line of the opposite character or through the projective bundles they define.

*Needs:* §§7.3, 5.2; SchemeAndStackFoundations, gap:stack-rational-chow; AlgebraicModuliForArithmeticGeometry §5.1.

**Checks.**

- A line bundle of nonzero `μ₄`-character, such as `O_{ℙS⁺}(−1)` restricted to a marked point, does not descend untwisted; the identity `c₁(L_i) = −ψ_i` of §7.6 is a statement about the descended twist determined by the canonical embedding.
- A rational Chow isomorphism for the base alone does not give the CKgP; the isomorphism must hold after product with every test stack.
- The projective bundle `ℙS⁺` descends to `BPSO₁₀` although `S⁺` does not.

### 7.5 The marked spinor incidence

On `Z_n`, for `0 ≤ n ≤ 4`, put `Q_n = coker(⊕_i L_i → S⁺)`, of rank `16 − n`, and let `G_n` be the Grassmann bundle of rank-nine locally free quotients of `Q_n` (AlgebraicModuliForArithmeticGeometry §1.3), equivalently of rank-`(7 − n)` subbundles `E_n ⊂ Q_n` with locally free quotient; the inverse image of `E_n` in `S⁺` is a rank-seven subbundle containing the lines `L_i`. The incidence `I_n` of §7.3 is the open locus of `G_n` where the corresponding linear section is a smooth curve (CLP, equations (5.11)–(5.13) and Lemma 5.13, pp. 20–21).

*Needs:* §§7.2–7.3; AlgebraicModuliForArithmeticGeometry §1.3 (`coherentGrassmannian` and `grassmannSmoothness`).

API:

- `rank Q_n = 16 − n`, `rank E_n = 7 − n`, and the universal quotient `Q_n/E_n` has rank nine.
- Forgetting a marked line compares the incidences for `n` and `n − 1` by the evident flag correspondence.
- After §7.3 the smooth-section locus is the pentagonal curve family with its markings.
- For a test scheme `T` over `Z_n`, points of `G_n` over `T` are locally free rank-nine quotients of `Q_n|_T` up to isomorphism of quotients, equivalently rank-`(7 − n)` subbundles with locally free quotient.
- The universal sequence `0 → E_n → Q_n → B_n → 0` and the inverse-image rank-seven subbundle of `S⁺` commute with base change.
- `G_n → Z_n` is smooth of relative dimension `9(7 − n)`.

**Checks.**

- `n = 0`: `G_0` is the Grassmannian of rank-nine quotients of a rank-sixteen space, equivalently of seven-dimensional subspaces, of dimension `63`.
- `n = 4`: `rank Q_4 = 12`, `rank E_4 = 3`, quotient rank nine.
- Two equal marked points give dependent lines and lie outside `Z_n`.
- The Grassmannian of rank-three quotients of `Q_4` has kernels of rank nine and cannot supply `E_4` of rank three; the rank-nine quotient convention is the right one.

### 7.6 Tautological Chern classes on the Mukai model

For `n ≤ 4`, after §7.4, prove: the bundle `U_i` restricted to `M°_{7,n}` sits in `0 → U_i → Sym² f_*(ω(−σ_i)) → f_*(ω(−σ_i)^{⊗2}) → 0`; `c₁(L_i) = −ψ_i` for the descended line of §7.4; and `0 → ⊕_i L_i → E^∨ → E_n → 0` with `E` the Hodge bundle, of ranks `n, 7, 7 − n`. Hence all Chern classes of `U_i`, `L_i` and `E_n` restrict to tautological classes on `M°_{7,n}` (CLP, Lemmas 5.14–5.16, pp. 21–22). Route: the relative quadric-multiplication sequence; the canonical embedding identifies the point line with `σ_i^*ω^∨`; the rank-seven span is the dual Hodge bundle; Grothendieck–Riemann–Roch for the universal curve.

*Needs:* §§7.4–7.5; §§0.2, 0.6; StableReductionPartII §5.1 (Hodge bundle); SchemeAndStackFoundations, gap:stack-rational-chow (Grothendieck–Riemann–Roch on stacks).

**Checks.**

- The sign of the point line is negative: `L_i = σ_i^*ω^∨` has `c₁ = −ψ_i`.
- `rank E_n = 7 − n`, not `16 − n`: for `n = 0` it is the dual Hodge bundle of rank seven.
- Ranks in the last sequence add up: `n + (7 − n) = 7`.

### 7.7 The Chow–Künneth generation property in genus seven

For `0 ≤ n ≤ 4` prove that `M°_{7,n}` has the CKgP and `A^*(M°_{7,n}) = R^*(M°_{7,n})`; for `0 ≤ n ≤ 3` prove that `M_{7,n}` has the CKgP and `A^*(M_{7,n}) = R^*(M_{7,n})` (CLP, Theorem 1.10, p. 4; Lemmas 5.13 and 5.17, pp. 20–22). Route: for `1 ≤ n ≤ 4`, §§7.4–7.6 and §7.2 give generation by tautological classes on the open subset `I_n` of `G_n`. For `n = 0`, the forgetful map `π : M°_{7,1} → M°_7` is proper and surjective, so §5.2 gives the CKgP, and `π_*(ψ₁ · π^*α) = κ₀ α = 12α` (§0.2) shows that every class is tautological; this avoids the empty fibre product excluded in §7.2. For `n ≤ 3` combine with §§6.2 and 6.8 by localization along the gonality strata.

*Needs:* §§7.2, 7.4–7.6; §§6.2, 6.8; §§5.2, 0.2, 0.6.

**Checks.**

- `n = 4` is a statement about the pentagonal locus only; `M_{7,4}` is not claimed, since §6.8 is limited to `n ≤ 3`.
- For `n = 0` the division by `κ₀ = 2·7 − 2 = 12` is legitimate over `ℚ`.
- The conclusion extends the ranges of §§5.4 and 6.9 to genus seven, `c(7) = 3`.

### Examples

At `n = 0` the Chow ring of `M°_7` is generated by `κ` and `λ` classes, being generated on the Mukai lift by Chern classes of `U`-type bundles and of the dual Hodge bundle.

### Dependencies

Layers 0, 5 and 6; `TauCeti.spinPlus` and companions; AlgebraicModuliForArithmeticGeometry §§1.3 and 5.1; StableReductionPartII §§0.4 and 5.1; SchemeAndStackFoundations, gap:stack-rational-chow; EquivariantIntersectionTheory §§2.2–2.3.

## Layer 8: Hyperelliptic counts and the genus-four quadric sieve

The sieve keeps singular and nonreduced complete intersections until their inverse-zeta contributions cancel. For a family `C → V` of curves over `𝔽_q` the sieve term `S_d(C) = Σ_{v ∈ V(𝔽_q)} s_d((C_v)^{sing})` sums the signed configuration coefficients of the singular loci of the fibres, `S₀(C) = #V(𝔽_q)`, and when the sieve applies (§8.7) the number of smooth fibres is `Σ_{d≥0} S_d(C)`. Sections 8.9–8.11 and 8.14 state the truncated sums `N^•_{n,≤3} = Σ_{d≤3} S_d(C^•_n)` divided by `#Aut(Q^•)`; these are sums of sieve terms, not counts of smooth fibres, and the count of smooth fibres adds the tail of §8.13. Throughout, `p_r = 1 + q + ⋯ + q^r = #ℙ^r(𝔽_q)`, `(x)_n = x(x − 1)⋯(x − n + 1)` is the falling factorial with `(x)_0 = 1`, and `q¹⁵` denotes `q^{15}`.

### 8.1 The hyperelliptic stack as a quotient of binary forms

For `g ≥ 2` the presentation of the stack `H_g` of smooth hyperelliptic curves of genus `g` as the quotient of the open subscheme `A_sm(1, 2g + 2)` of binary forms of degree `2g + 2` with nonvanishing discriminant by `G = GL₂/μ_{g+1}`, over a field of characteristic not dividing `4(g + 1)`, is EquivariantIntersectionTheory §3.3. Prove the same presentation over `ℤ[1/2]`, hence over every field of odd characteristic, including the characteristics dividing `g + 1`, agreeing with the supplier's where both apply (Arsie–Vistoli, Corollaries 4.2 and 4.7, which present the stack of smooth uniform cyclic double covers of Brauer–Severi curves with branch divisor of degree `2g + 2` as this quotient over `ℤ`, and Proposition 3.4 with Example 3.5(a), which identify that stack over `ℤ[1/2]` with the hyperelliptic locus of `M_g`); prove that `G` is smooth and connected over `ℤ`, isomorphic to `GL₂` for even `g` and to `𝔾_m × PGL₂` for odd `g` (Arsie–Vistoli, Proposition 4.4), so that `#G(𝔽_q) = (q² − 1)(q² − q)`; and prove `#H_g(𝔽_q) = q^{2g−1}` for odd `q` (BFP, §5, pp. 1332–1333). Route: Lang's theorem for the connected group `G` gives `#H_g(𝔽_q) = #P_g/#G(𝔽_q)`, where `P_g` is the set of squarefree polynomials of degree `2g + 2` or `2g + 1`; unique factorization as a squarefree part times the square of a monic polynomial gives `#P_g = (q − 1)(q^{2g+2} − q^{2g})`.

*Needs:* EquivariantIntersectionTheory §3.3 (the presentation away from the characteristics dividing `4(g + 1)`); StableReductionPartII §0.4; WeilConjectures, WC.1 (stack mass, quotients by connected groups via Lang's theorem).

**Checks.**

- `(q − 1)(q^{2g+2} − q^{2g}) / ((q² − 1)(q² − q)) = q^{2g−1}`: the factor `(q − 1)q^{2g}(q² − 1)` cancels against `(q² − 1)q(q − 1)`.
- The count is a groupoid mass, not the number of isomorphism classes: every hyperelliptic curve has the hyperelliptic involution, so a curve with no further automorphisms has weight one half, and it and its quadratic twist are distinct classes.
- The binary-form model requires `q` odd; characteristic two is §8.3.
- At `g = 4`, `q = 5` the supplier's hypothesis fails (`5` divides `4(g + 1) = 20`, and `μ₅` is not reduced over `𝔽₅`), while §8.3 needs `#H₄(𝔽₅) = 5⁷ = 78125`. Here `G = GL₂/μ₅ ≅ GL₂` is still smooth and connected, `#G(𝔽₅) = 24 · 20 = 480`, and `#P₄ = 4 · (5^{10} − 5⁸) = 37500000 = 480 · 78125`. For odd `g` the group is `𝔾_m × PGL₂`, with `(q − 1) · q(q² − 1) = (q² − 1)(q² − q)` points.

### 8.2 The first hyperelliptic Frobenius moments

For odd `q` and `g ≥ 2` let `a_C = q + 1 − #C(𝔽_q)`. Prove `Σ_{C ∈ [H_g(𝔽_q)]} a_C^k / #Aut(C)` equals `q^{2g−1}` for `k = 0`, `0` for odd `k`, and `q^{2g} − 1` for `k = 2` (BFP, Lemma 5.2, pp. 1333–1334). Route: twisting by a non-square exchanges `a_C` and `−a_C`, which kills odd moments; for `k = 2` write `a_C = −Σ_{x ∈ ℙ¹(𝔽_q)} χ(f(x))` with `χ` the quadratic character, separate the diagonal terms, which count forms not vanishing at `x`, from the off-diagonal terms, which vanish when summed over all forms of bounded degree by interpolation at two points and give `−#G(𝔽_q)` over squarefree forms by the squarefree–square factorization.

*Needs:* §8.1; `quadraticChar`, `quadraticChar_sum_zero`.

**Checks.**

- The second moment is `q^{2g} − 1`, not `q^{2g}`: the off-diagonal correlation over squarefree forms is `−#G(𝔽_q)`, not zero.
- Odd moments vanish as weighted sums; individual `a_C` need not be zero (a curve with `#C(𝔽_q) = q + 2` has `a_C = −1`).
- Summing `χ(f(x))χ(f(y))` over all binary forms of degree `d ≥ 1` with `x ≠ y` gives zero, because evaluation at two distinct points is surjective onto `𝔽_q²` and `Σ_a χ(a) = 0`.

### 8.3 Hyperelliptic counts with at most three markings

For every finite field and `g ≥ 2` prove `#H_g = q^{2g−1}`, `#H_{g,1} = (q + 1)q^{2g−1}`, `#H_{g,2} = (q + 2)q^{2g} − 1` and `#H_{g,3} = (q² + 3q − 1)q^{2g} − 3q`, the markings ordered and distinct (BFP, Proposition 5.1, p. 1333; Bergström, Theorem 10.3, for all characteristics). Route for odd `q`: `#H_{g,n} = Σ_C #C(#C − 1)⋯(#C − n + 1)/#Aut(C)` expanded in the moments of §8.2 with `#C = q + 1 − a_C`; for characteristic two use Bergström's computation.

*Needs:* §§8.1–8.2; WeilConjectures, WC.1.

**Checks.**

- At `g = 4, n = 0` the count is `q⁷`.
- For `n = 2` the factor is `#C(#C − 1)`, not `#C²`: `(q + 1)q·q^{2g−1} + (q^{2g} − 1) = (q + 2)q^{2g} − 1`.
- For `n = 3` the coefficient of `a_C²` in `N(N − 1)(N − 2)`, `N = q + 1 − a_C`, is `3q`, giving `(q³ − q)q^{2g−1} + 3q(q^{2g} − 1)`.

### 8.4 Quadric surfaces over finite fields

For every finite field `𝔽_q`, including characteristic two, prove that the geometrically reduced and geometrically irreducible quadric surfaces in `ℙ³` form three `PGL₄(𝔽_q)`-orbits: the cone `Q_con` over a smooth conic, isomorphic to `ℙ(1,1,2)`; the split smooth quadric `Q_spl ≅ ℙ¹ × ℙ¹`; and the non-split smooth quadric `Q_nsp`, which becomes `ℙ¹ × ℙ¹` over `𝔽_{q²}` with Frobenius exchanging the rulings. Prove that their stabilizers in `PGL₄(𝔽_q)` have orders `#Aut(Q_con) = q⁷ − q⁶ − q⁵ + q⁴`, `#Aut(Q_nsp) = 2(q⁶ − q²)` and `#Aut(Q_spl) = 2(q³ − q)²`, and that every automorphism of each surface is induced by `PGL₄` (BFP, §6 and equation (6), pp. 1334–1336). Route: classification of quadratic forms of rank three and four over finite fields, through the discriminant in odd characteristic and the Arf invariant in characteristic two; the stabilizers are `PGL₂(𝔽_q) × 𝔾_m ⋉ 𝔾_a³` for the cone, `(PGL₂ × PGL₂) ⋊ C₂` for the split quadric and `PGL₂(𝔽_{q²}) ⋊ C₂` for the non-split one. No roadmap states this classification; it is owned here.

*Needs:* Mathlib quadratic forms over fields (`QuadraticForm`); SchemeAndStackFoundations Layer 0 (projective space over a field).

**Checks.**

- `2(q³ − q)² = 2·#PGL₂(𝔽_q)²` and `2(q⁶ − q²) = 2·#PGL₂(𝔽_{q²})`; the factor two is the exchange of rulings, so dividing by `#PGL₂(𝔽_q)²` alone would double the split contribution.
- `q⁷ − q⁶ − q⁵ + q⁴ = q³ · q(q² − 1) · (q − 1)`: translations along the vertex direction, `PGL₂` on the conic, and scaling.
- A rank-two quadric `x₀² − εx₁²` with `ε` a non-square is irreducible over `𝔽_q` but geometrically a pair of conjugate planes; it is excluded, since the quadric of a canonical genus-four curve is geometrically irreducible.

### 8.5 Canonical genus-four quadric incidences

For a curve `C` of genus four over a perfect field `k` that is not geometrically hyperelliptic, the canonical morphism is a closed immersion into `ℙ³` onto the canonical model (CanonicalModelsAndGonality §§3.4 and 9.1), and over an algebraic closure the homogeneous ideal of that model is generated by an irreducible quadric, of rank three or four, and a cubic (CanonicalModelsAndGonality §3.9(b)); both are statements on function fields, read on curves through AlgebraicCurves Layer 12. Prove what the count adds: the quadrics containing the canonical model form the kernel of the `k`-linear map `Sym² H⁰(C, ω) → H⁰(C, ω^{⊗2})`, whose formation commutes with extension of `k` and which over `k̄` is spanned by the quadric of §3.9(b), so the quadric is unique, defined over `k` and geometrically irreducible; and conversely every smooth complete intersection of a quadric and a cubic in `ℙ³` is a canonically embedded curve of genus four (BFP, §6, pp. 1334–1335). Route for the converse: adjunction for the complete intersection gives `ω_C ≅ O_C(2 + 3 − 4) = O_C(1)`, of degree `6 = 2g − 2`, and the Koszul resolution gives `h⁰(O_C(1)) = 4 = g`, so the embedding is by the complete canonical system. For each type of §8.4 define the equation space `V^con ≅ 𝔸¹⁵` of complete intersections of `Q_con` with a cubic not passing through the vertex, equivalently degree-six curves in `ℙ(1,1,2)` with nonzero `w³` coefficient normalized to one; `V^nsp ≅ V^spl ≅ ℙ¹⁵` the linear system of cubic sections; for `n ≥ 0` the incidence `V^•_n ⊂ V^• × (Q^•)^n` of an equation together with `n` distinct ordered points on its zero curve; and `C^•_n → V^•_n` the universal curve, singular and nonreduced fibres included (BFP, §§7–8, pp. 1336–1340).

*Needs:* §8.4; StableReductionPartII §0.4 (genus-four curves); CanonicalModelsAndGonality §§3.4, 3.9 and 9.1 (Noether's theorem, the genus-four canonical ideal and the scheme form of the canonical embedding); AlgebraicCurves Layer 12 (function fields and smooth proper curves); SchemeAndStackFoundations §§2.20 and 3.3 (dualising sheaves, Riemann–Roch and Serre duality on curves, for the converse).

API:

- `V^•_n` maps to `V^•` by forgetting the ordered points.
- The incidence points are pairwise distinct; the fibre curve may be singular at them.
- The type is cone, non-split smooth or split smooth over `𝔽_q`.
- A marked point imposes one linear condition on the cubic section; a singularity at a point imposes three, and a singularity at a marked point two more.
- A geometric point of `V^•_n` is an allowed equation together with an ordered tuple of distinct points of its zero curve; singular or nonreduced zero curves are permitted.

**Checks.**

- `#V^con(𝔽_q) = q¹⁵` and `#V^nsp(𝔽_q) = #V^spl(𝔽_q) = p₁₅`.
- A cubic through the vertex of the cone is excluded from `V^con`: such a complete intersection is singular at the vertex.
- A curve with a double component is a point of `V^•`; excluding nonreduced fibres before sieving loses the cancellations of §8.7.

### 8.6 Counting genus-four curves by their quadric

For every `q` and `n ≤ 3` prove `#(M_{4,n} − H_{4,n})(𝔽_q) = Σ_• N^•_n(q)/#Aut(Q^•)`, the sum over the three types, where `N^•_n(q)` is the number of `𝔽_q`-points of `V^•_n` with smooth fibre (BFP, Propositions 6.1–6.2 and equation (6), pp. 1334–1336). Route: `M₄ − H₄` is the quotient of the space of smooth `(2,3)`-complete intersections by the connected group `PGL₄`, so its mass is a count divided by `#PGL₄(𝔽_q)`; the orbit–stabilizer formula for the `PGL₄(𝔽_q)`-orbit of the quadric, with the disconnected stabilizers of §8.4, replaces the global division.

*Needs:* §§8.4–8.5; WeilConjectures, WC.1 (stack mass and quotients by connected groups).

**Checks.**

- All denominators are nonzero for every prime power `q ≥ 2`; at `q = 2`, `#Aut(Q_spl) = 2·36 = 72`.
- Connected-group division is applied only to `PGL₄`; applying it directly to the disconnected stabilizers would lose the factor two.

### 8.7 Nonreduced fibres and termination of the sieve

Prove: the reduced singular locus of a nonreduced fibre of `C^con_n` is isomorphic to `ℙ¹`, with even Betti sum two; on `Q_nsp` it is a `(1,1)` curve, possibly with an isolated point, with even Betti sum at most four; on `Q_spl` it is a line of a ruling or a `(1,1)` curve, possibly with points, with even Betti sum at most five. Hence the signed configuration coefficients of these singular loci vanish in degrees above the Betti sum, and the Hasse–Weil sieve of WeilConjectures, WC.1 applies to each family (BFP, Examples 7.7–7.9, pp. 1339–1340). Nilpotent structure does not change the point counts or `ℓ`-adic cohomology used.

*Needs:* §8.5; WeilConjectures, WC.1 (signed Frobenius configurations, termination for proper even cohomology, the Hasse–Weil sieve).

**Checks.**

- For `ℙ¹` the inverse zeta function is `(1 − t)(1 − qt) = 1 − (q + 1)t + qt²`, of degree two: its coefficient of `t²` is `q ≠ 0`, while the coefficients of `t³, t⁴, …` vanish and the coefficients sum to zero at `t = 1`.
- Coefficient vanishing above the Betti sum is different from the cancellation of the total at `t = 1`; both are used.

### 8.8 Inverse zeta functions of the three quadric types

Prove that the smooth locus `Q_con − {vertex} = 𝔸² ⊔ 𝔸¹` has `Z^{−1} = (1 − qt)(1 − q²t)`, that `Z(Q_nsp)^{−1} = (1 − t)(1 − qt)(1 + qt)(1 − q²t)` and that `Z(Q_spl)^{−1} = (1 − t)(1 − qt)²(1 − q²t)` (BFP, equation (13), p. 1341; §8.2, p. 1343; §8.3, p. 1344). Signed configuration coefficients carry one sign for each Frobenius orbit, not for each geometric point.

*Needs:* §8.4; WeilConjectures, WC.1 (inverse zeta and configuration coefficients).

**Checks.**

- `s₂(Q_nsp) = 0` and `s₃(Q_nsp) = q⁴ + q²`, from `(1 − t)(1 − q²t)(1 − q²t²)`.
- `s₃(Q_spl) = −q⁴ − 2q³ − q²`.
- One orbit of degree two contributes `−t²` while two rational points contribute `+t²`: `#Q_nsp(𝔽_q) = q² + 1` and `#Q_nsp(𝔽_{q²}) = (q² + 1)²`, because the rulings are exchanged over odd-degree extensions and fixed over even ones.

### 8.9 Sieve coefficients on the quadric cone

Let `X = Q_con − {vertex}`, whose inverse zeta function `1 − (q² + q)t + q³t²` (§8.8) gives `s₁(X) = −q² − q`, `s₂(X) = q³` and `s_d(X) = 0` for `d ≥ 3`. For `0 ≤ n ≤ 3`, `d ≤ 3` and every finite field `𝔽_q`, prove that the sieve term `S_d(C^con_n)` is a polynomial in `q` (BFP, Propositions 8.1–8.2, pp. 1341–1343, which compute the sixteen terms one by one). Prove that `S₀(C^con_n) = (q² + q)_n q^{15−n}`, each marked point of `X(𝔽_q)` imposing one affine-linear condition on `V^con ≅ 𝔸¹⁵`. Without markings prove `S₁(C^con_0) = q¹² s₁(X) = −q¹⁴ − q¹³`, a singular point imposing three conditions; `S₂(C^con_0) = q⁹(s₂(X) − (q + 1)s₂(𝔸¹)) = q¹²`, because a fibre singular at two points of one ruling contains that ruling and hence the vertex, so that only the pairs off a common ruling contribute, each imposing six conditions; and `S₃(C^con_0) = 0`. Prove that the truncated sum `N^con_{n,≤3}` is divisible in `ℤ[q]` by `#Aut(Q_con) = q⁴(q − 1)²(q + 1)`, with quotient:

| `n` | `N^con_{n,≤3}/#Aut(Q_con)` |
| --- | --- |
| 0 | q⁸ |
| 1 | q⁹ + q⁸ − q⁷ |
| 2 | q¹⁰ + 2q⁹ − 2q⁸ − 2q⁷ + q⁶ − q⁵ |
| 3 | q¹¹ + 3q¹⁰ − 3q⁹ − 8q⁸ + 6q⁷ − 3q⁶ + 2q⁵ − 6q⁴ + 6q³ |

Route: stratify configurations of singular points by coincidences with marked points and by collinearity; a singularity imposes three linear conditions, a marked point one, a singular marked point two in addition to its marking condition; configurations containing a ruling line contribute multiples of `s₂(𝔸¹) = 0` or `s₃(ℙ¹) = 0`; count the remaining linear equation kernels, sum over `d ≤ 3` and divide.

*Needs:* §§8.5, 8.7, 8.8.

**Checks.**

- The unmarked terms sum to `q¹⁵ − q¹⁴ − q¹³ + q¹²`, and `(q¹⁵ − q¹⁴ − q¹³ + q¹²)/#Aut(Q_con) = q⁸`.
- `S₀(C^con_1) = q¹⁴(q² + q)`, since a marked point imposes one condition and `#Q_con^{sm}(𝔽_q) = q² + q`.
- `S₃(C^con_0) = 0`: three non-collinear singularities give `q⁶ s₃(X) = 0`, and collinear configurations contribute multiples of `s₂(𝔸¹) = 0`.
- Divisibility tests the four terms together: at `n = 1` they sum to `q¹⁶ − 3q¹⁴ + q¹³ + 2q¹² − q¹¹ = q¹¹(q − 1)²(q + 1)(q² + q − 1)`, and an error of one in a single coefficient adds `±q^k`, which `(q − 1)²(q + 1)` does not divide, so the quotient would not be a polynomial.

### 8.10 Sieve coefficients on the non-split quadric

For `0 ≤ n ≤ 3`, `d ≤ 3` and every finite field `𝔽_q`, prove that `S_d(C^nsp_n)` is a polynomial in `q` (BFP, Propositions 8.4–8.5, pp. 1343–1344, which compute the sixteen terms one by one). Without markings prove `S_d(C^nsp_0) = s_d(Q_nsp)·p_{15−3d}`, that is `p₁₅`, `−(q² + 1)p₁₂`, `0` and `(q⁴ + q²)p₆` for `d = 0, 1, 2, 3`, singularities at `d ≤ 3` points imposing `3d` independent conditions on `V^nsp ≅ ℙ¹⁵`; with markings prove `S₀(C^nsp_n) = (q² + 1)_n p_{15−n}`. With `#Aut(Q_nsp) = 2(q⁶ − q²)`, prove that `N^nsp_{n,≤3}/#Aut(Q_nsp)` equals the entry of the following table up to `o(q^{(9+n)/2})` for `n ≤ 2`, and up to `c q⁶ + o(q⁶)` with `c` a constant for `n = 3`, as `q` runs through prime powers:

| `n` | `N^nsp_{n,≤3}/#Aut(Q_nsp)` |
| --- | --- |
| 0 | ½(q⁹ − q⁶) |
| 1 | ½(q¹⁰ − q⁶) |
| 2 | ½(q¹¹ − q⁹ + q⁸ − q⁷ − q⁶) |
| 3 | ½(q¹² − 3q¹⁰ + 2q⁹ + 2q⁸ − 6q⁷) |

These are the precisions of §8.13; for `n = 3` the coefficient of `q⁶` merges with the integer `B_nsp` there. Route: as in §8.9; for `d = 3` with a singular hyperplane section through the three singularities, the section is a conjugate pair of lines through a rational point and the residual quadric section is counted separately; expand the quotient in descending powers of `q` through `1/(1 − q⁻⁴)`.

*Needs:* §§8.5, 8.7, 8.8.

**Checks.**

- The unmarked `S₂` vanishes, because `s₂(Q_nsp) = 0` and singularities at two points impose six independent conditions.
- Without markings the four terms sum to `q¹⁵ − q¹² − q¹¹ + q⁸ + q⁷ + q⁶ + q⁵ + q⁴`, whose quotient by `2(q⁶ − q²)` is `½(q⁹ − q⁶) + ½(q + 1) + O(q⁻¹)`; the part below degree `9/2` lies inside the error of §8.13 and is not part of the statement.
- The marking factor `(q² + 1)_n` counts ordered tuples of the `q² + 1` rational points of `Q_nsp`. Replacing it by the split factor `((q + 1)²)_n` changes `S₀(C^nsp_1)` by `2q·p₁₄` and the quotient at `n = 1` by `q⁹ + O(q⁸)`, which the stated `½(q¹⁰ − q⁶)` excludes.

### 8.11 Sieve coefficients on the split quadric

For `0 ≤ n ≤ 3`, `d ≤ 3` and every finite field `𝔽_q`, prove that `S_d(C^spl_n)` is a polynomial in `q` when `n = 0` or `d = 0`, and that for `1 ≤ n, d ≤ 3` it agrees with a polynomial in `q` up to `o(q⁶)` as `q` runs through prime powers (BFP, Propositions 8.6–8.7, pp. 1345–1346, which compute the sixteen terms one by one, the marked ones to that precision). Without markings prove `S_d(C^spl_0) = s_d(Q_spl)·p_{15−3d}`, that is `p₁₅`, `−(q + 1)²p₁₂`, `2(q³ + q² + q)p₉` and `−(q⁴ + 2q³ + q²)p₆`: two singularities impose six independent conditions, and three impose nine unless they lie on a line of a ruling, where they impose seven and contribute a multiple of `s₃(ℙ¹) = 0`. With markings prove `S₀(C^spl_n) = ((q + 1)²)_n p_{15−n}`. With `#Aut(Q_spl) = 2(q³ − q)²`, prove that `N^spl_{n,≤3}/#Aut(Q_spl)` equals the entry of the following table at the precisions of §8.10:

| `n` | `N^spl_{n,≤3}/#Aut(Q_spl)` |
| --- | --- |
| 0 | ½(q⁹ − q⁶) |
| 1 | ½(q¹⁰ + 2q⁹ − 2q⁷ − q⁶) |
| 2 | ½(q¹¹ + 4q¹⁰ + 3q⁹ − 5q⁸ − 3q⁷ − q⁶) |
| 3 | ½(q¹² + 6q¹¹ + 9q¹⁰ − 10q⁹ − 10q⁸ + 2q⁷) |

Route: as in §8.9; with markings, a singular `(1,1)` curve through two marked points imposes ten rather than eleven conditions; expand the quotient through `1/(1 − q⁻²)²`.

*Needs:* §§8.5, 8.7, 8.8.

**Checks.**

- The unmarked `S₂ = 2(q³ + q² + q)p₉` is nonzero, unlike the non-split case.
- `S₀(C^spl_1) = (q + 1)²p₁₄`, the number of rational points of `ℙ¹ × ℙ¹` times the cubic sections through one point.
- Without markings the split and non-split quotients agree to the stated precision, both `½(q⁹ − q⁶)`, although their terms differ (`S₂ = 2(q³ + q² + q)p₉` against `0`); below it they differ, `½(q⁹ − q⁶) − ½(q + 1) + O(q⁻¹)` against `½(q⁹ − q⁶) + ½(q + 1) + O(q⁻¹)`.
- With markings the terms are known only up to `o(q⁶)`; dividing by `2(q³ − q)²`, of degree six, leaves the quotient determined up to `o(1)`, inside the precision of §8.13. The quotients are sums of sieve terms, not counts of smooth fibres; the tail of §8.13 is added before any count is concluded.
- With §§8.3, 8.9 and 8.10, at `n = 1`: `(q⁹ + q⁸ − q⁷) + ½(q¹⁰ − q⁶) + ½(q¹⁰ + 2q⁹ − 2q⁷ − q⁶) + (q + 1)q⁷ = q¹⁰ + 2q⁹ + 2q⁸ − q⁷ − q⁶`, which is the open count of §9.6 in degrees five and above, as §8.6 and the bound `o(q⁵)` of §8.13 require.

### 8.12 A signed count of four points in general position

On `ℙ¹ × ℙ¹` over `𝔽_q` prove that the signed count of Frobenius-stable four-point configurations with no two points on a ruling and not all four on a `(1,1)` curve, each configuration weighted by `(−1)` to the number of its Frobenius orbits, equals `(q + 1)²q²(q − 1)² = #(PGL₂ × PGL₂)(𝔽_q)` (BFP, §8.4 and Remark 8.11, pp. 1349–1351). Route: subtract ruling and `(1,1)` configurations from the inverse-zeta coefficient `s₄`; independently, sum over the five Frobenius orbit types with their class sizes.

*Needs:* §8.8; WeilConjectures, WC.1 (signed configurations).

**Checks.**

- At `q = 2` the value is `9·4·1 = 36`.
- The count is signed and unordered; it is not the number of ordered four-tuples, which would be divisible by `4!`.

### 8.13 Contributions of four or more singularities

For each type and `0 ≤ n ≤ 2` prove `Σ_{d≥4} S_d(C^•_n)/#Aut(Q^•) = o(q^{(9+n)/2})`; for `n = 3` prove that there is an integer `B_•` with `Σ_{d≥4} S_d(C^•_3)/#Aut(Q^•) = B_• q⁶ + o(q⁶)` (BFP, Proposition 8.8, pp. 1346–1350). Route: nonreduced fibres by §8.7 and dimension counts; for reduced `(3,3)` curves classify the decompositions with four or more singularities, `(2,1) + (1,2)`, a singular `(2,2)` plus a `(1,1)`, `(3,2) + (0,1)`, and `(2,2) + (1,0) + (0,1)`, using §8.12 and genus-one counts with four to seven markings for the last family.

*Needs:* §§8.6–8.8, 8.12; §1.6 (genus-one counts).

**Checks.**

- For `n = 3` the integer `B_•` is not determined here; discarding it would produce the wrong coefficient of `q⁶` in §9.5, where it is fixed by an Euler characteristic.
- For `n ≤ 2` the bound `o(q^{(9+n)/2})` is exactly the precision required by the local cutoff in §9.3.

### 8.14 Sieve coefficients for nontrivial marking twists

For the cycle types `λ = (2), (2,1), (3)` let `C^•_λ → V^•_λ` be the twisted families of §1.1, in which geometric Frobenius permutes the markings by `σ(λ)`, and `N^•_{λ,≤3} = Σ_{d≤3} S_d(C^•_λ)`. Prove that the tail bounds of §8.13 hold for these families with `n = |λ|` (BFP, §11.2, p. 1357). Prove that `S₀(C^•_λ) = m^•_λ·e^•_{15−|λ|}`, where `e^con_r = q^r` and `e^nsp_r = e^spl_r = p_r` count the equations through the marked points, and `m^•_λ` counts the ordered marked configurations of type `λ` on the smooth locus: the ordered conjugate pairs `m^•_{(2)} = q⁴ − q`, `q⁴ + q²`, `q⁴ + q² − 2q` for the cone, the non-split and the split quadric; `m^•_{(2,1)} = m^•_{(2)}` times `q² + q`, `q² + 1`, `(q + 1)²` respectively; and the ordered conjugate triples `m^•_{(3)} = q⁶ + q³ − q² − q`, `q⁶ − q²`, `q⁶ + 2q³ − q² − 2q`. Prove that for `1 ≤ d ≤ 3` the term `S_d(C^•_λ)` is a polynomial in `q` for the cone and agrees with one up to `o(q⁶)` for the smooth quadrics (BFP, Propositions 11.2–11.4, pp. 1357–1360, which compute the thirty-six terms one by one), and that `S₂(C^nsp_{(3)}) = 0`: no singularity can be a marked point, the three marked points forming one Frobenius orbit, and two singularities off the marked points impose six conditions wherever they lie, so the term is a multiple of `s₂` of `Q_nsp` minus a closed point of degree three, which equals `s₂(Q_nsp) = 0` because removing that point multiplies the inverse zeta function by `(1 − t³)^{−1}`. Prove that `N^•_{λ,≤3}/#Aut(Q^•)` is given by the following table, exactly for the cone, and for the smooth quadrics up to `o(q^{11/2})` for `λ = (2)` and up to `c q⁶ + o(q⁶)`, `c` a constant, for `λ = (2,1), (3)`:

| Type | `λ = (2)` | `λ = (2,1)` | `λ = (3)` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| cone | q¹⁰ − q⁶ + q⁵ | q¹¹ + q¹⁰ − q⁹ − q⁶ + 2q⁵ − 2q⁴ | q¹¹ + q⁸ − q⁵ |
| non-split | ½(q¹¹ + q⁹ − q⁸ − q⁷ + q⁶) | ½(q¹² + q¹⁰ − 2q⁸ + 2q⁷) | ½(q¹² − q⁹ − q⁸) |
| split | ½(q¹¹ + q⁹ − 3q⁸ + q⁷ + q⁶) | ½(q¹² + 2q¹¹ + q¹⁰ − 4q⁹ − 2q⁸ + 2q⁷) | ½(q¹² − q⁹ − q⁸ + 2q⁷) |

Route: enumerate Frobenius orbit types of marked points and singular coincidences in the equation kernels, keeping the ruling involution on the non-split quadric; for cycle type `(3)` three collinear conjugate points need their own incidence count, and the twisted terms are not the untwisted ones with `q` replaced by `q³`.

*Needs:* §§1.1, 8.5, 8.8, 8.13.

**Checks.**

- A cycle type `(2)` marking has no rational fixed point: `S₀(C^con_{(2)}) = (q⁴ − q)q¹³ = q¹⁴(q³ − 1)`, the cubic sections through a degree-two orbit of smooth points; counting ordered rational pairs instead, `(q² + q)_2 q¹³`, gives the untwisted `S₀(C^con_2)`.
- The cone `(3)` coefficient `S₂` is `q¹⁵ − q¹³`, whereas the non-split `(3)` coefficient `S₂` is `0`.
- Evaluating characters: for `n = 2` the trivial and sign multiplicities of the twisted counts are recovered from the counts for `(1,1)` and `(2)` by `(f_{(1,1)} ± f_{(2)})/2`. For the cone, with §8.9, they are `q¹⁰ + q⁹ − q⁸ − q⁷` and `q⁹ − q⁸ − q⁷ + q⁶ − q⁵`; for the non-split quadric, with §8.10, `½(q¹¹ − q⁷)` and `½(−q⁹ + q⁸ − q⁶)` up to `o(q^{11/2})`.
- With §§9.2 and 9.9, at `λ = (2)`: `(q¹⁰ − q⁶ + q⁵) + ½(q¹¹ + q⁹ − q⁸ − q⁷ + q⁶) + ½(q¹¹ + q⁹ − 3q⁸ + q⁷ + q⁶) + (q⁹ − 1) = q¹¹ + q¹⁰ + 2q⁹ − 2q⁸ + q⁵ − 1`, while the open `S₂`-equivariant count of §9.9 at a transposition, its `s_{(2)}` polynomial minus its `s_{(1,1)}` polynomial, is `q¹¹ + q¹⁰ + 2q⁹ − 2q⁸`: the two agree in degrees six and above, the precision `o(q^{11/2})`.

### Examples

For `n = 0` the three quadric contributions are `q⁸` from the cone and, from the smooth quadrics, a total equal to `q⁹ − q⁶` up to `o(q^{9/2})`; adding `#H₄ = q⁷` gives `#M₄(𝔽_q) = q⁹ + q⁸ + q⁷ − q⁶`, the open count of §9.6.

### Dependencies

Layers 0 and 1; EquivariantIntersectionTheory §3.3; StableReductionPartII §0.4; SchemeAndStackFoundations Layer 0 and §§2.20, 3.3; CanonicalModelsAndGonality §§3.4, 3.9 and 9.1; AlgebraicCurves Layer 12; WeilConjectures, WC.1; Mathlib `quadraticChar`, `quadraticChar_sum_zero` and `QuadraticForm`.

## Layer 9: Genus-four point counts and odd vanishing through degree nine

The count recovers the Betti numbers of `M̄_{4,n}` only through the local cutoff for smooth proper stacks over `ℤ_p`; the splitting of the Galois representations comes from tautological generation (§9.7), not from the count. Coefficients of the open counts are virtual and can be negative.

### 9.1 The equivariant Euler characteristic of `M_{4,3}`

Prove that the rational Euler characteristic of `M_{4,3}` is `−10` and that its `S₃`-equivariant Euler characteristic is `2s_{(3)} − 6s_{(2,1)}`, with sign coefficient zero (BFP, §10.1, p. 1355, and §11.3, p. 1361, citing Gorsky). These are Euler characteristics of rational cohomology of the stack (equal to those of its coarse space), not orbifold Euler characteristics. Route: Gorsky's formula sums, over finite cyclic automorphism types of genus-four curves, contributions computed from the Harer–Zagier orbifold Euler characteristics of the quotient moduli spaces (Gorsky, Proposition 3.1, Definition 3.5, Lemma 3.6 and Theorem 3.8). Two misprints of the preprint text are corrected in its use here: the factor `½` in the genus of the quotient is misplaced in Theorem 3.8 (Kannan, Remark 3.8, notes this), and the genus is `h = 1 − (Σ_{j=1}^{r} k_j)/2`, including the negative exponent `k_r`, as in Gorsky's equation (3) and as Riemann–Hurwitz for the cyclic quotient requires: the sum runs to `r`, as Gorsky prints it, not to `r − 1` as Kannan's remark prints it (witness: the identity automorphism of a genus-four curve has `r = 1`, `k₁ = −6` and must give `h = 4`, which the sum to `r` gives and the sum to `r − 1` does not); and in the local congruence count of modulus `r` the symbol `k` in `gcd(x_i, k)` must be `r`. With these corrections the genus-four, degree-three coefficient is `−(5/3)p₁³ + p₁p₂ + (8/3)p₃` in power sums, which is `2s_{(3)} − 6s_{(2,1)} + 0·s_{(1,1,1)}`.

*Needs:* §1.2; StableReductionPartII §0.4; SchurWeyl, Layer 7 (power-sum and Schur bases).

**Checks.**

- At the identity, `2·1 − 6·2 = −10`: the ordinary value alone does not determine both Schur coefficients.
- For the identity automorphism of a genus-four curve the only exponent is `k₁ = −6`, and the corrected formula gives quotient genus `1 − (−6)/2 = 4`; the misprinted formula `(1 − Σk_j)/2` gives `7/2`, and a sum stopping at `r − 1 = 0` gives `1`. For the hyperelliptic involution of a genus-two curve the exponents are `k₁ = 6`, `k₂ = −4` (Gorsky, Remark 3.2, p. 4), and the formula gives `h = 1 − (6 − 4)/2 = 0`, the quotient `ℙ¹`; the sum to `r − 1` gives `−2` and the printed formula `−1/2`.
- Converting: `p₁³ = s_{(3)} + 2s_{(2,1)} + s_{(1,1,1)}`, `p₁p₂ = s_{(3)} − s_{(1,1,1)}`, `p₃ = s_{(3)} − s_{(2,1)} + s_{(1,1,1)}`; the combination `−(5/3)p₁³ + p₁p₂ + (8/3)p₃` has coefficients `2`, `−6`, `0`.

### 9.2 Equivariant hyperelliptic counts in genus four

For every `q` prove `#^{S₂}H_{4,2} = (q⁹ + q⁸ − 1)s_{(2)} + q⁸ s_{(1,1)}` and `#^{S₃}H_{4,3} = (q¹⁰ + q⁹ − q⁸ − q)s_{(3)} + (q⁹ − q)s_{(2,1)}`, with zero sign coefficient (BFP, §11.1, p. 1357, following Bergström, §§5–9 and Theorem 10.3). Route: count the twists `H_{4,λ}` of §1.1 for each cycle type and invert the character tables of `S₂` and `S₃`.

*Needs:* §§1.1, 1.2, 8.3; WeilConjectures, WC.1 (twists and the integral character lattice).

**Checks.**

- Evaluating at the identity recovers §8.3: `q⁹ + q⁸ − 1 + q⁸ = (q + 2)q⁸ − 1` and `(q¹⁰ + q⁹ − q⁸ − q) + 2(q⁹ − q) = (q² + 3q − 1)q⁸ − 3q`.
- The sign representation of `S₃` does not occur.

### 9.3 The genus-four cutoff

For `0 ≤ n ≤ 3`, if `#M̄_{4,n}(𝔽_{p^m}) = P(p^m) + o(p^{m(9+2n)/2})` for a fixed prime `p` and a polynomial `P ∈ ℚ[T]`, prove that `H^k(M̄_{4,n}) = 0` for every odd `k ≤ 9` (BFP, Corollary 3.2, p. 1330, whose printed conclusion names an arbitrary `M̄_{g,n}`; the conclusion is about `M̄_{4,n}`, and all genera follow only through §2.5). Route: apply the local cutoff of WeilConjectures, WC.5 to the smooth proper stack `M̄_{4,n}` of dimension `d = 9 + n` with `s = 9 + 2n ≥ d`: odd cohomology vanishes in degrees `k ≥ s`, and Poincaré duality turns this into degrees `k ≤ 2d − s = 9`. With the sharper error `o(q^{(9+n)/2})`, that is `s = d`, the same theorem gives all Betti numbers; this is the input of §9.5.

*Needs:* WeilConjectures, WC.5 (the local cutoff) and WC.6 (smooth proper DM purity); DeligneWeightsAndPurity §8.24 (smooth proper DM duality); StableReductionPartII §2.248.

**Checks.**

- At `n = 3` the cutoff exponent is `15/2`, whereas the exact count of §9.5 needs the sharper error `o(q⁶)`.
- `2d − s = 2(9 + n) − (9 + 2n) = 9` for every `n`.

### 9.4 Point counts of the boundary of `M̄_{4,n}`

Let `0 ≤ n ≤ 3`. If `n ≥ 2`, suppose that `#M_{4,1}(𝔽_q) = q¹⁰ + 2q⁹ + 2q⁸ − q⁷ − q⁶ − q²` for every `q`, and, if `n = 3`, also that `#M_{4,2}(𝔽_q) = q¹¹ + 3q¹⁰ + 4q⁹ − 2q⁸ − 4q⁷ − 2q³ − 2q²` for every `q`. Prove that `#∂M̄_{4,n}(𝔽_q)` is then, for every `q`, the polynomial of the following table, of degree `8 + n` (BFP, Proposition 9.6, pp. 1353–1354). Route: apply §1.4 to the counts of the open strata of smaller pairs. Those of genus at most three are the equivariant counts of §1.6. A vertex of genus four occurs only for `n ≥ 2`, as the vertex `(4, 1)` or `(4, 2)` of a graph with one edge to a rational vertex carrying the remaining legs; such a graph has no nontrivial automorphism, so the ordinary counts of the hypothesis suffice and no `S₂`-equivariant genus-four count enters (BFP, proof of Proposition 9.6, p. 1354). The hypothesis is discharged in §9.5, which proves the rows in the order of `n`.

| n | Polynomial |
| --- | --- |
| 0 | 1 + 4q + 13q² + 32q³ + 50q⁴ + 50q⁵ + 33q⁶ + 12q⁷ + 3q⁸ |
| 1 | 1 + 6q + 31q² + 93q³ + 191q⁴ + 240q⁵ + 192q⁶ + 94q⁷ + 28q⁸ + 4q⁹ |
| 2 | 1 + 11q + 78q² + 321q³ + 838q⁴ + 1362q⁵ + 1362q⁶ + 842q⁷ + 321q⁸ + 72q⁹ + 8q¹⁰ |
| 3 | 2 + 19q + 205q² + 1179q³ + 3977q⁴ + 8296q⁵ + 10606q⁶ + 8292q⁷ + 3990q⁸ + 1172q⁹ + 200q¹⁰ + 17q¹¹ |

*Needs:* §§1.4–1.6.

**Checks.**

- The `n = 3` constant coefficient is `2` and the coefficient of `q⁶` is `10606`, one more than the closed coefficient `10605`, so the open coefficient of `q⁶` is `−1`.
- Each row has leading coefficient equal to the number of boundary strata of dimension `8 + n`: `3` for `n = 0`, the divisors `Δ_irr, Δ₁, Δ₂`.

### 9.5 Polynomial counts of `M̄_{4,n}`

For `0 ≤ n ≤ 3` and every finite field prove that `#M̄_{4,n}(𝔽_q)` is the polynomial of the following table, of degree `d = 9 + n`, with nonnegative coefficients satisfying `a_i = a_{d−i}` (BFP, Theorem 1.4, p. 1325; §10.1, p. 1355). The count is the groupoid mass of the Conventions, `stableMass`: the sum of `1/#Aut` over the isomorphism classes of stable `n`-pointed curves of genus four over `𝔽_q`, which are the pointed prestable curves satisfying `IsStableOfGenus`; the table is `stableMass_genus_four`. `IsStableOfGenus` expresses stability as the absence of infinitesimal automorphisms, which over a field `k` is equivalent to it: a stable curve has none (StableReduction, Layer 3); an unstable connected prestable pointed curve has, after extension to an algebraic closure, a component whose normalization is rational with at most two special points (the preimages of nodes, a node of the component itself giving two, and the markings), or is a smooth genus-one curve without markings, and so carries a nonzero vector field vanishing at the markings, since a vector field on that normalization vanishing at its special points descends through the nodes and extends by zero to the other components; and the infinitesimal automorphisms over `k` form the `k`-space of such vector fields, whose formation commutes with extension of the field. Route: prove the rows in the order `n = 0, 1, 2, 3`, discharging the hypothesis of §9.4 at `n` by the open counts `#M_{4,n′} = #M̄_{4,n′} − #∂M̄_{4,n′}` of the rows `n′ < n` already proved. Add the cone, non-split and split counts of §§8.6, 8.9–8.11 and 8.13, the hyperelliptic counts of §8.3 and the boundary counts of §9.4; this determines `#M̄_{4,n}` up to `o(q^{(9+n)/2})` for `n ≤ 2` and up to `B q⁶ + o(q⁶)` for `n = 3`; WeilConjectures, WC.5 (approximate counts and Tate semisimplification, with the stack trace, comparison, purity and duality inputs) recognizes an exact palindromic polynomial, and for `n = 3` the coefficient of `q⁶` is fixed by `χ(M_{4,3}) = −10` (§9.1).

| n | Polynomial |
| --- | --- |
| 0 | 1 + 4q + 13q² + 32q³ + 50q⁴ + 50q⁵ + 32q⁶ + 13q⁷ + 4q⁸ + q⁹ |
| 1 | 1 + 6q + 30q² + 93q³ + 191q⁴ + 240q⁵ + 191q⁶ + 93q⁷ + 30q⁸ + 6q⁹ + q¹⁰ |
| 2 | 1 + 11q + 76q² + 319q³ + 838q⁴ + 1362q⁵ + 1362q⁶ + 838q⁷ + 319q⁸ + 76q⁹ + 11q¹⁰ + q¹¹ |
| 3 | 1 + 21q + 207q² + 1168q³ + 3977q⁴ + 8296q⁵ + 10605q⁶ + 8296q⁷ + 3977q⁸ + 1168q⁹ + 207q¹⁰ + 21q¹¹ + q¹² |

*Needs:* §§8.3, 8.6, 8.9–8.11, 8.13, 9.1, 9.4; WeilConjectures, WC.5 and WC.6; DeligneWeightsAndPurity §8.24 (smooth proper DM duality); StableReduction, Layer 3 (stability, and the absence of infinitesimal automorphisms on a stable geometric fibre, for `IsStableOfGenus`); StableReductionPartII §0.4 (the groupoid of `𝔽_q`-points of `M̄_{4,n}`, for `stableMass`).

API:

- For a pointed prestable curve `P` over a field `k`, `IsStableOfGenus k g P` holds exactly when the base change of `P` to an algebraic closure is stable in the sense of StableReduction, Layer 3, and `dim_k H¹(P, 𝒪_P) = g`; it depends only on the isomorphism class of `P` over `k`.
- For a finite field `k`, the stable `n`-pointed curves of genus `g` over `k` have finitely many isomorphism classes, each with a finite automorphism group, and `stableMass k g n` is the finite groupoid mass (WeilConjectures, WC.1) of their groupoid: it is `#M̄_{g,n}(k)` for a stable pair `(g, n)`, and `0` for `(0, n)` with `n ≤ 2` and for `(1, 0)`, which admit no stable curve.

**Checks.**

- `IsStableOfGenus k 0` holds for `ℙ¹_k` marked at `0, 1, ∞`: a vector field vanishing at the three markings is a section of `T_{ℙ¹}(−3) = O(−1)`, hence zero, and `h¹(O) = 0`. It fails for `ℙ¹_k` marked at `0, ∞`, where `z∂_z` is a nonzero infinitesimal automorphism. `IsStableOfGenus k 1` fails for the unmarked irreducible rational curve with one node, to which `z∂_z` descends (on the node `xy = 0` it acts by `x ↦ x`, `y ↦ −y`); a test asking only that smooth rational components carry three special points would accept that curve.
- `stableMass 𝔽_q 0 4 = q + 1`: the `q − 2` smooth classes and the three boundary classes all have trivial automorphism group, in agreement with `#M̄_{0,4}(𝔽_q)` of §1.6.
- `stableMass 𝔽_q 1 1 = q + 1`: the smooth curves contribute the mass `q` of §1.1, and the split and the non-split nodal cubic each have an automorphism group of order two, the involution of the normalization that fixes the marking and exchanges the two preimages of the node. Counting isomorphism classes instead gives `2q + O(1)` (§1.1, third Check).
- `n = 0`: `1, 4, 13, 32, 50, 50, 32, 13, 4, 1`, symmetric of degree nine.
- The middle coefficient `10605` for `n = 3` is fixed by the Euler characteristic, not by palindromicity: the coefficient of `q⁶` is its own mirror.
- Closed minus boundary equals the open table of §9.6 in every row.

### 9.6 Polynomial counts of `M_{4,n}`

For `0 ≤ n ≤ 3` and every `q` prove that `#M_{4,n}(𝔽_q)` is the polynomial of the following table, the difference of §9.5 and §9.4, whose hypothesis at `n` is the rows `n′ < n` of this table (BFP, Theorem 1.5, p. 1326; §10.1, p. 1355). In the `n = 3` row the coefficient `−11` belongs to `q³`; the display in BFP §10.1 places it at `q⁴`, contrary to Theorem 1.5 and to the subtraction.

| n | Polynomial |
| --- | --- |
| 0 | −q⁶ + q⁷ + q⁸ + q⁹ |
| 1 | −q² − q⁶ − q⁷ + 2q⁸ + 2q⁹ + q¹⁰ |
| 2 | −2q² − 2q³ − 4q⁷ − 2q⁸ + 4q⁹ + 3q¹⁰ + q¹¹ |
| 3 | −1 + 2q + 2q² − 11q³ − q⁶ + 4q⁷ − 13q⁸ − 4q⁹ + 7q¹⁰ + 4q¹¹ + q¹² |

*Needs:* §§9.4–9.5.

**Checks.**

- `n = 0`: `q⁹ + q⁸ + q⁷ − q⁶`, and its value at `q = 1` is `χ(M₄) = 2`.
- `n = 3` at `q = 1` gives `−10 = χ(M_{4,3})`.
- Open coefficients can be negative; they are the weight-graded Euler characteristic of `H_c^*`, not Betti numbers.

### 9.7 Pure Tate cohomology of `M̄_{4,n}`

For `n ≤ 3` prove that `H^*(M̄_{4,n}) = RH^*(M̄_{4,n})`, and hence that `H^{odd}(M̄_{4,n}) = 0` and `H^{2j}(M̄_{4,n}) ≅ L^{j ⊕ a_{n,j}}`, with `a_{n,j}` the coefficient of `q^j` in §9.5, as rational Hodge structures and as `ℚ_ℓ[G_ℚ]`-modules, without semisimplification (BFP, Theorem 1.4, pp. 1325–1326, for the pure Tate cohomology and its Betti numbers; Canning–Larson, Theorem 1.4, p. 4, for tautological generation, which covers `g = 4`, `n ≤ 6`, and Proposition 3.15, p. 13, for its arithmetic consequence). Route: every pair `(g′, n′) ≼ (4, n)` lies in the ranges of §5.4 (`g′ = 4`, `n′ ≤ 3`; `g′ = 3`, `n′ ≤ 5`; `g′ = 2`, `n′ ≤ 7`; `g′ = 1`, `n′ ≤ 9`; genus zero), so `W_kH^k(M_{g′,n′}) = RH^k(M_{g′,n′})` there in both realizations; induction on the stable-pair order with §0.4, Künneth on the normalized boundary strata and closure of tautological classes under clutching pushforward gives `H^*(M̄_{4,n}) = RH^*(M̄_{4,n})`. Tautological classes are cycle classes of cycles defined over `ℚ`, so `H^{2j}` is spanned by classes of Hodge type `(j, j)` and, after the twist `(j)`, by `G_ℚ`-invariant vectors: it is a sum of copies of `L^j` as a Hodge structure and as a Galois representation, since a representation spanned by vectors on which `G_ℚ` acts through one character is a sum of copies of that character. The local cutoff of WeilConjectures, WC.5, applied to the exact count of §9.5, gives `dim H^{2j} = a_{n,j}`. The clause of WC.5 on polynomial counts over all of `Spec ℤ` is not used: its Deligne–Mumford version rests on the `p`-adic comparison for smooth proper stacks of WeilConjectures, WC.1, which this roadmap does not consume, and a characteristic polynomial of Frobenius alone would not split a global extension.

*Needs:* §§0.1, 0.3, 0.4, 5.4, 9.5; StableReductionPartII §3.10 (the normalized boundary strata); MotivesAndAlgebraicCycles, gap:dm-stack-cycle-class; WeilConjectures, WC.5 (the local cutoff) and WC.6; DeligneWeightsAndPurity §8.24 (Künneth and smooth proper DM duality, over finite fields) with WeilConjectures, WC.1 (stack Artin comparison and smooth proper transport, for the `G_ℚ`-realization); MixedHodgeModulesAndIrregularHodgeTheory, gap:dm-stack-mixed-hodge.

**Checks.**

- The Chow–Künneth ranges, not the point count, give the splitting. At `n = 3` the largest genus-one pair below `(4, 3)` is `(1, 9)`, with `2·1 + 9 = 11 = 2·4 + 3`, inside `c(1) = 10`; the largest genus-two pair is `(2, 7)`, inside `c(2) = 10`. From the count alone WeilConjectures, WC.5 (approximate counts and Tate semisimplification) gives only `H^{2j}(M̄_{4,n})^ss ≅ ℚ_ℓ(−j)^{⊕ a_{n,j}}`.
- The route through all pairs `≼ (4, n)` reaches `n = 4` (largest pairs `(1, 10)`, `(2, 8)`, `(3, 6)`, all inside the ranges) and stops at `n = 5`: `(1, 11) ≼ (4, 5)`, since `2 + 11 = 13 = 8 + 5`, and `M_{1,11}` lies outside the Chow–Künneth range, `W₁₁H¹¹(M_{1,11}) = S₁₂` being non-algebraic (§5.4).
- `H¹²(M̄_{4,3})` has dimension `10605` and is a sum of copies of `L⁶`, of Hodge type `(6, 6)`.
- For the open stacks the tables describe only `e_c`: `M_{4,0}` has `H_c^*` with a virtual class `−L⁶` in weight twelve.

### 9.8 Odd cohomology vanishes through degree nine

For every stable pair prove `H^k(M̄_{g,n}) = 0` for odd `k ≤ 9` (BFP, Theorem 1.1, p. 1324; §2, pp. 1328–1329). The bound is sharp uniformly in `(g, n)`, since `dim H¹¹(M̄_{1,11}) = 2`. Route: §2.5 with `k = 9`; genus zero is §2.6, and the base region consists of the pairs of §2.9 and of `(4, n)`, `n ≤ 3`, where §9.7 applies; §2.2 keeps genus five out of the region.

*Needs:* §§2.2, 2.5, 2.6, 2.8, 2.9, 9.7; §0.5.

**Checks.**

- The statement concerns all genera and markings; §9.3 alone concerns genus four.
- `H¹¹(M̄_{1,11}) ≅ S₁₂ ≠ 0` shows that nine cannot be replaced by eleven.

### 9.9 Symmetric-group equivariant counts in genus four

For `n = 2, 3` prove that the `S_n`-equivariant counts of `M̄_{4,n}` and `M_{4,n}` are given by the following Schur-coefficient polynomials; compact coefficients are classes of actual Betti representations and open coefficients are virtual (BFP, Theorem 11.1, p. 1356, and §11.3, pp. 1361–1362). Route: evaluate the twisted counts of §§8.14 and 9.2 and the equivariant boundary counts of §1.4 at each cycle type, invert the character tables, impose equivariant palindromicity and, for `n = 3`, the equivariant Euler characteristic of §9.1. The compact coefficients are actual representations: by the local cutoff (WeilConjectures, WC.5) applied to the exact count of §9.5, every Frobenius eigenvalue on `H^{2j}(M̄_{4,n} ⊗ 𝔽̄_q)` is `q^j`, and `σ` commutes with Frobenius, so the trace identity of §1.5 gives the count of the twist by `σ` as `Σ_j tr(σ | H^{2j}) q^j`, with no semisimplicity of Frobenius; and palindromicity is `S_n`-equivariant Poincaré duality on `M̄_{4,n}`, every rational representation of `S_n` being self-dual. The clause of WeilConjectures, WC.5 on equivariant polynomial counts, whose Deligne–Mumford version needs local `p`-adic inputs, is not used.

- At `q = 1` the open `S₃` class is `2s_{(3)} − 6s_{(2,1)}` with zero sign part, matching §9.1.
- Each compact Schur polynomial is palindromic, as required for self-dual irreducibles of `S_n`.

### 9.10 Euler characteristics of local systems on `M₄`

For each partition `λ` with `|λ| ≤ 3` listed below, prove that the alternating trace of geometric Frobenius on `H_c^*(M₄ ⊗ 𝔽̄_q, V_λ)` is the polynomial in the table, for every `q`; its weight-graded Euler characteristic is obtained by replacing `q` with `L` (BFP, Theorem 11.5, p. 1362). Route: relative Künneth for the fibre powers of the universal curve over `M₄` and the decomposition of tensor powers of `V` (§3.1) express `#^{S_m}M_{4,m}` for `m ≤ 3` through these traces with the Tate twists of the symplectic contractions; solve using §9.9.

| Highest weight | Alternating compact-support trace |
| --- | --- |
| `(1)` | q² + q⁷ |
| `(1,1)` | −q² − q⁷ − q⁸ + q⁹ |
| `(2)` | 0 |
| `(1,1,1)` | −q² − q⁴ + q⁶ + q⁸ − q⁹ − q¹⁰ |
| `(2,1)` | 1 + 2q³ − q⁴ − q⁷ + q⁸ |
| `(3)` | −1 − 2q + q³ |

*Needs:* §§3.1, 9.6, 9.9.
