- The trace for `λ = (2)` is `0`; this is the vanishing of an Euler characteristic, not of every `H_c^i(M₄, V_{(2)})`.
- `n = 1`: `(q⁹ + q⁸ + q⁷ − q⁶)(q + 1) − (q⁷ + q²) = q¹⁰ + 2q⁹ + 2q⁸ − q⁷ − q⁶ − q²`, the open count of §9.6.
- The traces for `(1,1)` and `(2)` differ, because `Λ²V` contains the contraction `L` and `Sym²V` does not.

### Examples

`#M̄₄(𝔽₂) = 1 + 8 + 52 + 256 + 800 + 1600 + 2048 + 1664 + 1024 + 512 = 7965`. The middle Betti number of `M̄_{4,3}` is `10605`.

### Dependencies

Layers 0–3 and 8; Layer 5 (§5.4, for §9.7); StableReduction, Layer 3; StableReductionPartII §§0.4, 2.248 and 3.10; WeilConjectures, WC.1, WC.5, WC.6; DeligneWeightsAndPurity §8.24; MixedHodgeModulesAndIrregularHodgeTheory with gap:dm-stack-mixed-hodge; MotivesAndAlgebraicCycles with gap:dm-stack-cycle-class; SchurWeyl, Layer 7.

## Layer 10: Even cohomology, even homology and the eleventh cohomology group

Cohomological and homological statements now diverge: low even homology controls high complementary cohomology, and it does not make every low even cohomology group tautological. The same boundary induction with Chow–Künneth base cases, run in degree eleven with the second differential of the oriented boundary complex, shows that `H¹¹` vanishes outside genus one.

### 10.1 Tautological cohomology through degree three

For every stable pair prove `H^k(M̄_{g,n}) = RH^k(M̄_{g,n})` for `0 ≤ k ≤ 3`: `H⁰ = ℚ`, `H¹ = H³ = 0`, and `H²` is spanned by the boundary divisor classes, the psi classes and `κ₁` (AC, Theorems (2.1) and (2.2); CLP, §6.1, p. 22). Route: §2.7 for odd degrees; the Arbarello–Cornalba induction for `H²`, with base cases from §1.6 and the stable range §3.4.

*Needs:* §§0.1, 2.7, 1.6, 3.4; §0.4.

**Checks.**

- `H²(M̄_{0,4}) = ℚ`, spanned by any of the three boundary points.
- An even Tate realization alone does not identify classes as tautological; the theorem identifies explicit generators.
- `dim H²(M̄_{1,1}) = 1`, spanned by `ψ₁ = δ_irr/12`.

### 10.2 All degree-four cohomology is tautological

For every stable pair prove `H⁴(M̄_{g,n}) = RH⁴(M̄_{g,n})` (CLP, Theorem 1.5(1), p. 2; §6.1, p. 22). Route: §4.9 with `k = 4` reduces to `g < 7` and `n ≤ 4`, where §§5.4 and 6.9 make the pure open quotient tautological; apply §4.5 to `RH^*`, including the same-degree triples with fewer markings, with §10.1 for lower degrees.

*Needs:* §§4.5, 4.9, 5.3, 5.4, 6.9, 10.1.

**Checks.**

- Genus seven does not occur in the seed region: the genus bound `g′ < 3·4/2 + 1 = 7` is strict.
- For `(g, n) = (6, 4)`, `n = 4 ≤ c(6) = 5`, so §6.9 applies.

### 10.3 Degree-six cohomology in genus at least ten

For `g ≥ 10` and every `n` prove `H⁶(M̄_{g,n}) = RH⁶(M̄_{g,n})` (CLP, Theorem 1.5(2), p. 2; §6.1, p. 22). Route: `W₆H⁶(M_{g,n})` is tautological by §3.5; boundary classes are pushforwards of degree-four classes and of products of lower degrees, tautological by §§10.1–10.2; apply §0.4.

*Needs:* §§0.4, 3.5, 10.1, 10.2.

**Checks.**

- No statement is made for `g ≤ 9`; §10.6 gives a criterion there.
- `6 ≤ (2g − 2)/3` holds exactly when `g ≥ 10`.

### 10.4 Tautological even homology through degree fourteen

For every stable pair and every even `0 ≤ k ≤ 14` prove that `H^{2d−k}(M̄_{g,n})` is tautological, equivalently that `H_k(M̄_{g,n})` is spanned by tautological cycles; groups of negative cohomological degree are zero (CLP, Theorem 1.5(3), p. 2; §6.2, p. 23). Route: induct on `k` and on the stable-pair order with §0.4. By §2.3, `H_k^{BM}(M_{g,n}) = 0` unless `n ≤ 1, 2g ≤ k` or `n ≥ 2, 2g − 2 + n ≤ k`; for `g ≥ 3` and `k ≤ 14` all those pairs lie in the ranges of §§5.4, 6.9 and 7.7, so the pure Borel–Moore classes are tautological by §5.3; genus one is §3.3 and genus two §4.3; boundary Künneth factors have even homological degree, because by §9.8 and Poincaré duality odd homology vanishes below degree eleven and two odd degrees at least eleven cannot sum to at most fourteen. The first sentence of the proof in CLP §6.2 writes the range as `k ≥ 14`; the theorem and the argument use `k ≤ 14`.

*Needs:* §§0.4, 2.3, 2.6, 3.3, 4.3, 5.3, 5.4, 6.9, 7.7, 9.8.

**Checks.**

- The statement is not `H^k = RH^k` for even `k ≤ 14`; that is the conjecture of CLP, Conjecture 1.8, for `k ≥ 6`.
- At `k = 14` the genus-seven pairs `(7, 0)`, `(7, 1)` (with `2g = 14`) and `(7, 2)` (with `2g − 2 + n = 14`) are needed, which is why §7.7 is a prerequisite; genus eight does not occur, since `2g = 16 > 14`.
- For `g = 3` the pairs `n ≤ 11` with `2g − 2 + n ≤ 14` are within `c(3) = 11`.

### 10.5 The complementary degree-fourteen realization

For every stable pair prove `H^{2d−14}(M̄_{g,n})^ss ≅ L^{d−7 ⊕ m}` for some multiplicity `m` depending on the pair, as rational Hodge structures and as `ℚ_ℓ[G_ℚ]`-modules, with `m = 0` when `2d − 14` lies outside `[0, 2d]` (CLP, Corollary 6.2, p. 23). Route: algebraic classes of codimension `d − 7` span a sum of `L^{d−7}`.

*Needs:* §10.4; MotivesAndAlgebraicCycles, gap:dm-stack-cycle-class.

**Checks.**

- The exponent is `d − 7`; the group `H¹⁴` itself has Tate exponent `7` by Poincaré duality (§12.9).
- For `M̄_{0,10}`, `d = 7`, so `H⁰ = L⁰ = ℚ`.

### 10.6 A finite criterion for degree-six cohomology

Prove: if the intersection pairing `RH⁶(M̄_{g,n}) × RH^{2d−6}(M̄_{g,n}) → ℚ(−d)` is perfect for every stable pair with `g ≤ 9` and `n ≤ 6`, then `H⁶(M̄_{g,n}) = RH⁶(M̄_{g,n})` for every stable pair (CLP, Remark 6.1, p. 22). Route: by §10.4, `H^{2d−6} = RH^{2d−6}`; perfectness and Poincaré duality give `dim H⁶ = dim RH⁶`, hence `H⁶ = RH⁶`, at the pairs of the finite range. Apply §4.5 to `S = RH^*` in degree six. Its degree-six triples have vanishing quotient: in the finite range by the hypothesis, `W₆H⁶(M_{g′,n′})` being a quotient of `H⁶(M̄_{g′,n′})` by §0.4; for `g′ ≥ 10` by §10.3; for `2 ≤ g′ ≤ 9` and `n′ ≥ 6` by §4.2; in genus one by §3.2, `W₆H⁶(M_{1,n′}) = 0` because `S₇ = 0`; and in genus zero by §2.6. Its triples of degree at most five have vanishing quotient by §10.1 (degrees at most three), §10.2 (degree four) and §2.7 (degree five), through the same surjection of §0.4. The finite perfectness hypothesis is not asserted.

*Needs:* §§0.4, 2.6, 2.7, 3.2, 4.2, 4.5, 10.1, 10.2, 10.3, 10.4.

**Checks.**

- The paired degrees are `6` and `2d − 6`; pairing `RH⁶` with itself is meaningful only when `d = 6`.
- For `k ≥ 22` the analogous pairing is not perfect in general (Petersen–Tommasi, recalled in CLP, p. 3), so the criterion is specific to degree six.
- For `(g, n) = (2, 7)`, §4.5 calls on the triple `(1, 7, 6)`: `(1, 7) ≺ (2, 7)` and `2d_{1,7} − 6 = 8 ≤ 2d_{2,7} − 6 = 14`. It lies outside the finite range only in its marking count and outside §§4.2 and 10.3 by its genus; its quotient vanishes by §3.2, `W₆H⁶(M_{1,7}) = 0`. The degree-four triple `(1, 7, 4)` needs §10.2.

### 10.7 The oriented weight-`k` boundary complex

For a stable pair `(g, n)` and `k ≥ 0` let `C^j_k(g, n) = ⊕_{|E(Γ)|=j} (H^k(M̄_Γ) ⊗ det ℚ^{E(Γ)})^{Aut Γ}`, the sum over isomorphism classes of stable graphs `Γ` of type `(g, n)` with `j` edges, `M̄_Γ = ∏_v M̄_{g_v,n_v}`; so `C⁰_k(g, n) = H^k(M̄_{g,n})`, and `C^j_k(g, n) = 0` for `j > d_{g,n}`. Define `d_j : C^j_k → C^{j+1}_k` as the sum, over `(j + 1)`-edge graphs `Γ′` and their edges `e`, of the pullbacks along the clutchings `M̄_{Γ′} → M̄_{Γ′/e}` that glue the two flags of `e`, with the orientation `e ∧ (orientation of Γ′/e)`; write `α = d₀` and `β = d₁`. Prove that `d_{j+1} d_j = 0`, and that `C^•_k(g, n)` is the weight-`k` row of the `E₁` page of the weight spectral sequence for the compactly supported cohomology of `M_{g,n} ⊂ M̄_{g,n}`, which degenerates at `E₂`, so that `ker α = Gr^W_k H_c^k(M_{g,n})` and `ker β / im α = Gr^W_k H_c^{k+1}(M_{g,n})`: over finite fields by DeligneWeightsAndPurity §8.24, in the Hodge realization by its analogue, gap:dm-stack-mixed-hodge, and in the `G_ℚ`-realization over `ℚ̄` by the comparison of the Conventions (CLP-H11, §4.1, pp. 10–11; CLP, equations (8.1)–(8.2), p. 28, for `k = 15`). The vertex factors are compact moduli, not open strata.

*Needs:* DeligneWeightsAndPurity §8.24 (over finite fields); WeilConjectures, WC.1 (stack Artin comparison and smooth proper transport, for the `G_ℚ`-realization); MixedHodgeModulesAndIrregularHodgeTheory, gap:dm-stack-mixed-hodge; StableReduction, Layer 1 (stable graphs); StableReductionPartII §§3.1–3.2 and 3.10 (clutching and the boundary normalization with graph automorphisms); §0.4.

API:

- `d_{j+1} d_j = 0`: the two orders of contracting two edges of a `(j + 2)`-edge graph enter with opposite signs.
- `α : H^k(M̄_{g,n}) → C¹_k(g, n)` is pullback to the normalized one-edge boundary strata, taken on graph-automorphism invariants.
- `ker α = Gr^W_k H_c^k(M_{g,n})` and `ker β / im α = Gr^W_k H_c^{k+1}(M_{g,n})`; in particular `α` is injective exactly when `Gr^W_k H_c^k(M_{g,n}) = 0`.
- Reordering the edges of a summand by a permutation `τ` multiplies its orientation by `sign(τ)`; an automorphism of `Γ` acts on `det ℚ^{E(Γ)}` by the sign of the permutation it induces on the edges.
- The complex is `S_n`-equivariant and its differentials are morphisms of Hodge structures and of Galois representations.

**Checks.**

- For `(0, 3)` there are no edges: `C^•_0 = ℚ` in degree zero, `α = 0`, and `ker α = ℚ = Gr^W_0 H_c^0(M_{0,3})`, `M_{0,3}` being a point.
- For `(0, 4)` and `k = 0`: `C⁰_0 = H⁰(ℙ¹) = ℚ`, `C¹_0 = ℚ³` from the three boundary points, there are no two-edge graphs, and `α` is the diagonal; so `ker α = 0 = Gr^W_0 H_c^0(M_{0,4})` and `C¹_0 / im α = ℚ² = Gr^W_0 H_c^1(M_{0,4})`, `M_{0,4}` being `ℙ¹` minus three points.
- The orientation character matters: on `M̄_{1,2}`, the stratum of two rational vertices joined by two edges, each carrying one leg, has an edge exchange acting trivially on `H⁰` and by `−1` on `det ℚ^E`, so its summand of `C²_0(1, 2)` is zero, while plain invariants would give `ℚ` (compare §1.5).

### 10.8 Vanishing of `H¹¹` in genus two

For every `n ≥ 0` prove `H¹¹(M̄_{2,n}) = 0` (CLP-H11, §4.3, pp. 11–14, and Lemma 3.4, p. 9). Route: for `n ≤ 10`, `M_{2,n}` has the CKgP (§5.4), so `W₁₁H¹¹(M_{2,n}) = 0`, and the boundary term `H⁹(D̃)(−1)` of §0.4 vanishes, the normalized boundary strata being products of moduli spaces of genus at most two (§2.9); so `H¹¹(M̄_{2,n}) = 0` by §0.4. For `n = 11`, §4.6 with `(n, k) = (11, 11)`, where `T^{11}_{11} = 0`, puts `H¹¹(M̄_{2,11})` in the STE generated by `H¹¹(M̄_{2,10}) = 0`, `H⁹(M̄_{2,10}) = 0` and `H⁹(M̄_{1,m}) = 0`, which is `RH^*`, with zero degree-eleven part. For `n ≥ 12` induct on `n`. Since `2g − 2 + n > 11`, §2.3 gives `H_c^{11}(M_{2,n}) = 0` and `α` is injective (§10.7). By Künneth and §2.9, `H¹¹` of a product of vertex spaces is the sum of their `H¹¹`. The summands of `C¹_{11}(2, n)` with a genus-two vertex vanish by induction. The remaining one-edge graphs are the loop graph and the separating graphs with summand `H¹¹(M̄_{1,A∪{p}}) ⊕ H¹¹(M̄_{1,A^c∪{q}})`, `|A^c| ≤ |A|`; order them as the loop graph, then `|A^c| = 0`, then `|A^c| = 1`, then `|A^c| ≥ 2`. The loop summand `H¹¹(M̄_{1,n+2})^{S₂}` injects under `β` into the two-edge graphs `Γ_B` obtained from the divisors `D_B ⊂ M̄_{1,n+2}`, `|B| = 10`, of §4.7 (which applies, `n + 2 ≥ 14`) by gluing the loop flags `p₁, q₁`. If `|A^c| ≤ 1`, the factor `M̄_{1,A^c∪{q}}` has at most two flags and no `H¹¹`, and `M̄_{1,A∪{p}}` has `|A| + 1 ≥ n ≥ 12` flags, so §4.7 injects its `H¹¹` into the two-edge graphs obtained from its divisors `D_B`, `|B| = 10`; the other contraction of such a graph has a genus-two vertex when `p ∈ B`, and when `p ∉ B` it is a separating graph whose genus-one vertices carry the ten markings of `B` and the `n − 10 ≥ 2` others, which comes later. If `|A^c| ≥ 2`, then `|A| ≥ 2`; choose `i, j ∈ A^c` and `k, l ∈ A`, and let `Φ_A` be the two-edge graph splitting a rational vertex carrying `i, j` off the vertex of `A^c ∪ {q}`, and `Φ_{A^c}` the one splitting `k, l` off the vertex of `A ∪ {p}`. The factor `M̄_{1,A∪{p}}` is a factor of `M̄_{Φ_A}`, and the component of `β` from `H¹¹(M̄_{1,A∪{p}})` to this summand of `H¹¹(M̄_{Φ_A})` is the identity up to the orientation sign, while `H¹¹(M̄_{1,A^c∪{q}})` maps to the other summand; symmetrically for `Φ_{A^c}`. So this case does not use the injectivity of §4.7, which fails for a factor with exactly eleven flags (`|A| = 10`). The other contraction of `Φ_A` and of `Φ_{A^c}` has a genus-two vertex (CLP-H11, §4.3.2 and §4.3.3, Cases 1–3, pp. 12–14). Thus no two-edge graph used contracts to an earlier one-edge graph with nonzero `H¹¹`, `β` is block upper triangular with injective diagonal blocks, hence injective, and `β α = 0` gives `H¹¹(M̄_{2,n}) = 0`.

*Needs:* §§0.4, 2.3, 2.9, 4.6, 4.7, 5.3, 5.4, 10.7.

**Checks.**

- `(2, 9)` is a base case: `2g − 2 + n = 11`, so §2.3 does not give `H_c^{11}(M_{2,9}) = 0`; instead `9 ≤ c(2) = 10` gives `W₁₁H¹¹(M_{2,9}) = 0`, and the boundary term of degree nine vanishes.
- At `(2, 11)` the generators of §4.6 have degrees `11` on `(2, 10)` and `9` on `(2, 10)` and `(1, m)`, `m ≤ 13`, all zero, and `T^{11}_{11} = 0`.
- At `(2, 12)` the separating graph with `|A| = 10` and `A^c = {i, j}` has the eleven-flag factor `M̄_{1,A∪{p}} ≅ M̄_{1,11}`, whose restriction to its own boundary is zero (§4.7), and a three-flag factor `M̄_{1,{i,j,q}}`. It is detected through `Φ_A`, which splits the rational vertex carrying `i, j` off the three-flag factor: the vertices of `Φ_A` are `M̄_{1,11}`, `M̄_{1,{q,p′}}` and `M̄_{0,{q′,i,j}}`, and `β` is, up to sign, the identity on the rank-two summand `H¹¹(M̄_{1,11})`. Splitting a rational vertex off the eleven-flag factor instead would detect nothing.
- For `p₁ ∈ B`, `q₁ ∉ B` the edge exchange `σ` of `Γ_B` transposes two of the eleven points of the genus-one factor, so it acts on `H¹¹(M̄_{Γ_B})` by `−1`, and on `det ℚ^E` by `−1`; hence `(H¹¹(M̄_{Γ_B}) ⊗ det ℚ^E)^{Aut} = H¹¹(M̄_{Γ_B})`, and the component of `β` is `ξ₁^* − σ^*ξ₁^* = 2ξ₁^*`. Without the orientation character the invariants would be zero and this summand would detect nothing.

### 10.9 The eleventh cohomology group in all genera

Prove that `H¹¹(M̄_{g,n}) ≠ 0` if and only if `g = 1` and `n ≥ 11`, and that the STE `S_ω` generated by `H¹¹(M̄_{1,11})` contains `H¹¹(M̄_{g,n})` for every stable pair (CLP-H11, Theorem 1.1, p. 1, and §4.2, p. 11; recalled in CLP, §2.2, p. 7). Route: genus zero is §2.6, genus one §§4.7–4.8, genus two §10.8. For `g ≥ 3` induct on `g` and then on `n`. The pairs with `max(2g, 2g − 2 + n) ≤ 11` and `g ≥ 3` are `(3, n ≤ 7)`, `(4, n ≤ 5)` and `(5, n ≤ 3)`; each `M_{g,n}` there has the CKgP (§§5.4, 6.9), so `W₁₁H¹¹(M_{g,n}) = 0`, and the boundary term of degree nine vanishes by §9.8, so `H¹¹(M̄_{g,n}) = 0` by §0.4. Otherwise §2.3 gives `H_c^{11}(M_{g,n}) = 0` and `α` is injective (§10.7). By Künneth and §9.8, `H¹¹` of a product of vertex spaces is the sum of their `H¹¹`; by induction the only nonzero summands of `C¹_{11}(g, n)` come from separating edges joining a vertex of genus `g − 1 ≥ 2` to a genus-one vertex with at least eleven flags, a graph `Γ` with trivial automorphism group. Adding a loop at the genus-`(g − 1)` vertex gives a two-edge graph `Γ′` whose `H¹¹` contains the same genus-one summand; the other contraction of `Γ′` is the one-vertex loop graph of type `(g − 1, n + 2)`, whose `H¹¹` vanishes by induction, and distinct `Γ` give distinct `Γ′`, so `β` is injective. Then `β α = 0` gives `H¹¹(M̄_{g,n}) = 0`.

*Needs:* §§0.4, 2.3, 2.6, 4.7, 4.8, 5.3, 5.4, 6.9, 9.8, 10.7, 10.8.

**Checks.**

- The degree-eleven base region in genus at least two is `(2, n ≤ 9)`, `(3, n ≤ 7)`, `(4, n ≤ 5)`, `(5, n ≤ 3)`: `max(2g, 2g − 2 + n) = 11` at `(2, 9)`, `(3, 7)`, `(4, 5)`, `(5, 3)`, and genus six has `2g = 12 > 11`. All lie in the CKgP ranges `c(2) = 10`, `c(3) = c(4) = 11`, `c(5) = 7`; a genus-one-only base region would miss them.
- The boundary of `M̄_{5,3}` has the loop stratum with vertex `(4, 5)`, of genus four, so the degree-nine vanishing needs §9.8 and not only §2.9.
- At `(3, 12)` the map `α` lands in a nonzero space: the separating graph joining a genus-two vertex with one leg to a genus-one vertex with eleven legs contributes `H¹¹(M̄_{1,12})`, of dimension `22`. The loop at the genus-two vertex gives `Γ′`, whose other contraction is of type `(2, 14)`, with `H¹¹(M̄_{2,14}) = 0` by §10.8.

### Examples

`H⁴(M̄_{2,0})` is spanned by tautological classes; `M̄₂` has `b₄ = 2`. For `M̄_{0,n}` every statement of this layer is §2.6. `H¹¹(M̄_{3,12}) = 0`, although `C¹_{11}(3, 12)` contains a copy of `H¹¹(M̄_{1,12}) ≠ 0`.

### Dependencies

Layers 0–7 and 9; MotivesAndAlgebraicCycles with gap:dm-stack-cycle-class; DeligneWeightsAndPurity §8.24 with WeilConjectures, WC.1; MixedHodgeModulesAndIrregularHodgeTheory with gap:dm-stack-mixed-hodge; StableReductionPartII §§3.1–3.2, 3.10; StableReduction, Layer 1.

## Layer 11: Cusp-decorated graphs and the exceptional genus-two bases

Graphical bases count independent morphisms from the rank-two seed `S₁₂`. For `H¹⁵(M̄_{2,12})` the boundary restrictions detect `825` of the `836` copies; the remaining eleven are found through a nonzero pairing and an independent upper bound.

### 11.1 Galois realizations in degrees thirteen and fifteen in genus two

For every `n ≥ 0` prove that, as `ℚ_ℓ[G_ℚ]`-modules, `W₁₃H¹³(M_{2,n})^ss` and `H¹³(M̄_{2,n})^ss` are sums of copies of `L S₁₂`, and `W₁₅H¹⁵(M_{2,n})^ss` and `H¹⁵(M̄_{2,n})^ss` are sums of copies of `L² S₁₂`, multiplicities zero allowed (CLP, Proposition 2.8 and Corollary 2.9, p. 8). Route: the surjection `H^k(M̄_{2,n}) → W_kH^k(M_{2,n})` factors through the compact-type moduli space, whose cohomology decomposes into cohomology of symplectic local systems on `M₂^{ct}` and on `Sym² M_{1,1}` (Petersen, genus two, Theorem 2.1), the latter restricting to zero; local systems of odd weight have no cohomology because of the hyperelliptic involution, and for even weight Petersen's classification of the cohomology of local systems on the moduli of abelian surfaces (Petersen, abelian surfaces, Theorem 2.1) leaves only `L S₁₂` in pure weight thirteen and `L² S₁₂` in pure weight fifteen; boundary terms are controlled by §§4.7, 10.9, 9.8 and 10.1. In degree thirteen the normalized boundary contributes only `H¹¹` of genus-one factors. In degree fifteen it contributes `H¹¹` of a genus-one factor tensored with `H²` of the other factor, and `H¹³` of single factors. Genus-zero factors have no odd cohomology (§2.6), and a genus-two factor is covered by the degree-thirteen statement, proved first. A genus-one factor `M̄_{1,m}` has `W₁₃H¹³(M_{1,m}) = 0` by §3.2, since `S₁₄ = 0`, so by §0.4 its `H¹³` is a quotient of `H¹¹` of its own normalized boundary twisted by `L`, hence a sum of copies of `L S₁₂` by §§2.9 and 4.7. The Hodge version of the degree-fifteen statement is §12.5.

*Needs:* §§0.4, 0.5, 2.6, 2.9, 3.1, 3.2, 3.6 (the Torelli identification `M₂^{ct} ≅ A₂` and the locus `Sym² M_{1,1}`), 4.7, 9.8, 10.1, 10.9; PELModuli §M5.2 (the stack `A₂` and its local systems `V_{a,b}`).

**Checks.**

- The statement concerns semisimplifications of Galois representations; it gives no splitting and does not deduce Hodge types from Frobenius polynomials.
- Petersen's theorem is used beyond the range `a + b ≤ 12` of §3.6. In degree fifteen the compact-type decomposition contains `H^{15−q}(M₂^{ct}, A_q)` for every even weight `q ≤ 14`, and for `n ≥ 14` the local system `A₁₄` contains `V_{a,b}` with `a + b = 14`; the term is `H¹(A₂, V_{a,b})`, Poincaré dual to `H⁵_c(A₂, V_{a,b})`, which vanishes because `H^k_c(A₂, V_{a,b}) = 0` for `k ∉ {2, 3, 4}` when `(a, b) ≠ (0, 0)` (Petersen, abelian surfaces, Theorem 2.1(1), p. 4). In degree thirteen the weights are `q ≤ 12`.
- `S₁₆` does not occur in genus two: the only weight-fifteen pure piece allowed by the abelian-surface classification is `L² S₁₂`.

### 11.2 Cusp-decorated stable graphs

An `ω`-decorated stable graph of type `(g, n)` is a stable graph `Γ` with `n` legs and `e` edges, a designated genus-one vertex `v₀` with an ordered set of eleven of its flags, and on each vertex `v` a tautological decoration, a class of `R^{c_v}(M̄_{g_v,n_v})` of pure Chow codimension `c_v`; write `c = Σ_v c_v`, so that unit decorations have `c = 0`. Pushforward along the clutching morphism of `Γ` of the product of the decorations with the pullback of `ω ∈ H¹¹(M̄_{1,11})` along the forgetful map retaining the eleven chosen flags of `v₀`, extended linearly over `S₁₂`, defines a morphism `L^{e+c} S₁₂ → H^{11+2e+2c}(M̄_{g,n})`. For odd `k ≥ 11` a family of such graphs, each with `e + c = (k − 11)/2`, is a graphical basis of `H^k(M̄_{g,n})` when the corresponding morphisms form a basis of `Hom(L^{(k−11)/2} S₁₂, H^k(M̄_{g,n}))` and the evaluation map `Hom(L^{(k−11)/2} S₁₂, H^k) ⊗ L^{(k−11)/2} S₁₂ → H^k` is an isomorphism (CLP, §7.2, conventions 1–5, pp. 24–25, where every decoration is a unit and the exponent is the number of edges).

*Needs:* §§4.8, 0.5, 0.1; StableReductionPartII §§3.1–3.2.

API:

- A graph with its ordered eleven-flag set and decorations of total codimension `c` determines the morphism `L^{e+c} S₁₂ → H^{11+2e+2c}(M̄_{g,n})`; with unit decorations this is `L^e S₁₂ → H^{11+2e}(M̄_{g,n})`.
- Each edge, and each unit of Chow codimension of a decoration, adds two to the cohomological degree and one Tate twist `L`.
- The decorations `ω_i`, `ω_{ij}` are pullbacks along the maps forgetting the flags `i`, respectively `i, j`, the remaining flags of the genus-one vertex being retained in a fixed order.
- A basis of `r` morphisms gives `H^k ≅ (L^{(k−11)/2} S₁₂)^{⊕r}` when evaluation is an isomorphism.
- Permuting the eleven retained flags multiplies the morphism by the sign of the permutation, since `S₁₂` is the sign-isotypic part of `H¹¹(M̄_{1,11})`; relabelling the legs carries retained and forgotten flags along.

**Checks.**

- A two-edge graph with unit decorations contributes in degree `15` with source `L² S₁₂`.
- Decorations count in the degree and the twist. On the zero-edge graph of type `(1, 12)` with `ω_i` on its vertex, multiplying by a tautological divisor `a` that is the pullback of an ample class (gap:pointed-coarse-projectivity) gives `aω_i ≠ 0` in `H¹³`, since `d = 12` and cup product with `a` is an isomorphism `H¹¹ → H¹³` by hard Lefschetz. So `(e, c) = (0, 1)`, the degree is `13` and the source is `L S₁₂`, the same `Hom(L S₁₂, H¹³)` as the one-edge graphs of §11.3; counting edges only would put it in degree `11` with source `S₁₂`.
- Eleven independent one-edge morphisms give a subspace of rational dimension `22`, not `11`.
- A genus-one vertex with fewer than eleven flags carries no `H¹¹` decoration, since `H¹¹(M̄_{1,m}) = 0` for `m ≤ 10`.

### 11.3 A graphical basis of `H¹³(M̄_{1,12})`

Prove `H¹³(M̄_{1,12}) ≅ (L S₁₂)^{⊕11}` with graphical basis the eleven one-edge graphs whose rational tail carries the markings `1` and `i`, `2 ≤ i ≤ 12`, the genus-one vertex being decorated by `ω` on its eleven remaining flags (CLP, Lemma 7.4, p. 25). Route: pair against the basis `ω₂, …, ω₁₂` of `H¹¹(M̄_{1,12})` (§4.7); the pairing matrix is block diagonal with nonzero blocks; the multiplicity is `11` by Poincaré duality, `H¹³(M̄_{1,12}) ≅ H¹¹(M̄_{1,12})^∨(−12)` with `d = 12`, and §4.7, since `S₁₂^∨(−12) ≅ S₁₂(11)(−12) = L S₁₂`.

*Needs:* §§11.2, 3.2, 4.7; DeligneWeightsAndPurity §8.24 (smooth proper DM duality); MixedHodgeModulesAndIrregularHodgeTheory, gap:dm-stack-mixed-hodge (Poincaré duality, Hodge).

**Checks.**

- Rational dimension `22`.
- `S₁₄ = 0`, so `W₁₃H¹³(M_{1,12}) = 0` and the whole group is pushed forward from the boundary.

### 11.4 A graphical basis of `H¹³(M̄_{1,13})`

With markings `{x, y, 1, …, 11}`, prove `H¹³(M̄_{1,13}) ≅ (L S₁₂)^{⊕429}` with graphical basis the one-edge graphs, all unnamed markings on the genus-one vertex: (a) a rational tail with any three markings and `ω` on the eleven remaining flags of the genus-one vertex (`286`); (b) a tail `{y, i}`, `1 ≤ i ≤ 11`, with `ω_x` (`11`); (c) a tail `{x, j}`, `j ≠ x`, with `ω_i` for a marking `i ∉ {x, j}` (`132`) (CLP, Lemma 7.5, p. 26). Route: §3.2 puts the group on the boundary; modulo family (a), families (b) and (c) are pulled back from `H¹³(M̄_{1,12})`, whose relations (§11.3) move a fixed marking onto the rational vertex; the multiplicity `429` is §3.6, so spanning gives a basis.

*Needs:* §§11.2, 11.3, 3.2, 3.6.

**Checks.**

- `286 + 11 + 132 = 429`, with `286 = binom(13, 3)`; rational dimension `858`.
- The retained flags of every graph are named, so that the sign convention of §11.2 fixes each morphism.

### 11.5 A graphical basis of `H¹³(M̄_{1,14})`

With markings `{x, y, 1, …, 12}`, prove `H¹³(M̄_{1,14}) ≅ (L S₁₂)^{⊕6006}` with graphical basis five families of one-edge graphs, the attaching flag retained and all unnamed markings on the genus-one vertex: (a) a four-marking tail with `ω` (`1001`); (b) a three-marking tail with `ω_l` for one of the eleven markings on the genus-one vertex (`4004`); (c) a tail `{1, i}`, `2 ≤ i ≤ 12`, with `ω_{xy}` (`11`); (d) a tail `{y, j}`, `1 ≤ j ≤ 12`, with `ω_{xi}`, `i ∈ {1, …, 12} − {j}` (`132`); (e) a tail `{x, k}`, `k ≠ x`, with `ω_{ij}` for an unordered pair `{i, j}` of the twelve markings other than `x` and `k`, so that `i`, `j` or `k` may be `y` (`858`) (CLP, Lemma 7.6, p. 26). Route: as in §11.4, straightening through `H¹³(M̄_{1,12})`; the multiplicity `6006` is §3.6, so spanning gives a basis.

*Needs:* §§11.2–11.4, 3.2, 3.6.

**Checks.**

- `1001 + 4004 + 11 + 132 + 858 = 6006`, with `1001 = binom(14, 4)` and `4004 = binom(14, 3)·11`; rational dimension `12012`.
- `858 = 13·66`: thirteen choices of `k` and `binom(12, 2) = 66` pairs. Restricting `i, j` to the markings `1, …, 12` would give `66` pairs for `k = y` but only `binom(11, 2) = 55` for each of the twelve `k ∈ {1, …, 12}`, a total of `66 + 12·55 = 726`; similarly in family (b) a tail containing `x` or `y` leaves eleven markings on the genus-one vertex, not all of them among `1, …, 12`.

### 11.6 A graphical basis of `H¹³(M̄_{2,12})`

Prove `H¹³(M̄_{2,12}) ≅ (L S₁₂)^{⊕264}`, with graphical basis the `264` one-edge graphs joining two genus-one vertices, the right vertex carrying a pair `{i, j}`, a single marking `j`, or no original marking, with the left decoration forgetting zero, one or two flags accordingly (`66 + 132 + 66`) (CLP, Lemma 7.7, p. 26). Route: pull back to `M̄_{1,14}` by replacing the right genus-one vertex by a rational vertex with two new markings `x, y`; the images are distinct members of the basis of §11.5, hence independent; the multiplicity `H¹³(M̄_{2,12})^ss ≅ (L S₁₂)^{⊕264}` is §3.6, so the `264` independent morphisms from the simple object `L S₁₂` span.

*Needs:* §§11.2, 11.5, 3.6.

**Checks.**

- `66 + 132 + 66 = 264`; rational dimension `528`.
- Independence is detected after pullback; spanning follows from the multiplicity `264`, not from the pullback.

### 11.7 The rank of the 891-graph restriction matrix

Let `R` be the `891` two-edge `ω`-decorated graphs of CLP Figure 2 for `H¹⁵(M̄_{2,12})`, in families of sizes `132, 11, 132, 220, 66, 66, 132, 66, 66`, and consider the matrix of selected Künneth components of their pullbacks to boundary divisors. Prove that it has rank `825` and that its relations are spanned by the `66` combinations `M_{ij} = R₁(ij) + (1/24)R₂(ij) + R₃[i, j] + R₃[j, i] − R₄(ij) + (1/12)R₅(ij)`, indexed by unordered pairs `{i, j}` of markings (CLP, Figure 2, p. 25, and proof of Lemma 7.3, pp. 26–27). These are relations among the selected restrictions, not by themselves relations among the global classes. Route: family `A₁` is alone in the first column; families `A₂`–`A₄` have independent entries in the second column by §11.4, so the `495` rows of `A₁`–`A₄` are independent of each other and of the rest; the last four families (`66, 132, 66, 66` rows) are block upper triangular, so the rank is at least `495 + 330 = 825`. The `66` relations hold by the tautological relations in `H⁴(M̄_{1,3})`, `H²(M̄_{1,2})` and `H²(M̄_{1,1})`, with the excess-intersection signs, and are linearly independent, the row `R₅(ij)` occurring in `M_{ij}` alone; so the rank is at most `891 − 66 = 825`.

*Needs:* §§11.2, 11.4–11.6, 0.1, 3.3.

**Checks.**

- `132 + 11 + 132 + 220 + 66 + 66 + 132 + 66 + 66 = 891` and `891 − 825 = 66`.
- The lower bound uses only the first four and the last four families: `132 + 11 + 132 + 220 = 495` and `66 + 132 + 66 + 66 = 330`, sum `825`. Treating the fifth family (`66` rows) as triangular too would give rank `891`, contradicting the `66` relations.
- The two-edge pullbacks contain negative psi decorations from the self-intersection of the boundary; omitting them changes the coefficients `1/24` and `1/12`.

### 11.8 The representation spanned by the relation symbols

Prove that the `S₁₂`-module spanned by the formal symbols `M_{ij}` is `Ind_{S₁₀ × S₂}^{S₁₂}(sgn ⊠ 1)`, of dimension `66`, isomorphic to `T_{(2,1^{10})} ⊕ T_{(3,1^9)}`, of dimensions `11` and `55` (CLP, proof of Lemma 7.3, p. 27, where the inducing subgroup is printed as `S₁₀`; induction from `S₁₀` alone has dimension `132`). Route: the ten retained flags outside `{i, j}` carry the sign character, the unordered pair is fixed; the Pieri rule adds a horizontal strip of two boxes to the column `(1^{10})`.

*Needs:* §11.7; SchurWeyl, Layer 7 (Pieri rule) and Layer 5 (hook dimensions).

**Checks.**

- `12!/(10!·2!) = 66` while `12!/10! = 132`.
- `dim T_{(2,1^{10})} = binom(11, 1) = 11`, `dim T_{(3,1^9)} = binom(11, 2) = 55`, and `11 + 55 = 66`.
- Adding two boxes in one new column would give `(1^{12})`, which is not a horizontal strip and does not occur.

### 11.9 Boundary generation of `H¹⁵(M̄_{2,12})`

With `H¹⁵(M̄_{2,12})^ss ≅ (L² S₁₂)^{⊕836}` from §3.6, prove that `H¹⁵(M̄_{2,12})` is in fact semisimple, equal to `(L² S₁₂)^{⊕836}`, spanned by the `891` morphisms of §11.7, whose global relations form a space of dimension `55`; hence `H¹⁵(M̄_{2,12})` lies in the STE generated by `H¹¹(M̄_{1,11})` (CLP, Lemma 7.3 and equation (7.1), pp. 24–27). Route: each `M_{ij}` is nonzero, by pairing with `R₅(ik)`, `k ≠ j`, with dual cusp decorations, where the first five terms vanish because no genus-one vertex with fewer than eleven flags carries `H¹¹` and the last is nonzero; so the image of the module of §11.8 in `Hom(L² S₁₂, H¹⁵(M̄_{2,12}))` is a nonzero quotient of `T_{(2,1^{10})} ⊕ T_{(3,1^9)}`, of dimension `11`, `55` or `66`; the span of the `891` morphisms has dimension `825` plus that dimension and is at most `836`, which excludes `55` (`880`) and `66` (`891`), so the image is the `11`-dimensional constituent; `825 + 11 = 836` independent rank-two morphisms give an actual direct sum.

*Needs:* §§11.7, 11.8, 3.6.

**Checks.**

- `825 + 11 = 836` and `891 − 836 = 55`; the constituent of dimension `55` would give `825 + 55 = 880 > 836`, and the whole module `891 > 836`.
- Rank `825` alone does not suffice; the nonzero pairing and the upper bound `836` are both used.
- The conclusion is an actual decomposition, not only an equality of semisimplifications: `836` independent morphisms from the simple object `L² S₁₂` into a group whose semisimplification has `836` copies.

### 11.10 The adjacent genus-two bases

Prove that the STE generated by `H¹¹(M̄_{1,11})` contains `H¹³(M̄_{2,11})` and `H¹⁵(M̄_{2,13})` (CLP, Lemma 7.1, p. 23, and Lemma 7.10, p. 27). Route: `M_{2,10}` has the CKgP (§5.4), so `H¹³(M̄_{2,10})` is pushed forward from the boundary and lies in the STE by §§4.8 and 10.9; then §4.6 with `n = 11, k = 13`. For `H¹⁵(M̄_{2,13})` apply §4.6 with `n = 13, k = 15`, using §11.9 for `H¹⁵(M̄_{2,12})`, §11.6 for `H¹³(M̄_{2,12})` and §4.8 for genus one.

*Needs:* §§4.6, 4.8, 5.4, 10.9, 11.6, 11.9.

**Checks.**

- The two applications of §4.6 use different lower groups: `(2, 10)` in degrees `13, 11` and `(2, 12)` in degrees `15, 13`.
- `n = 11` and `n = 13` are odd, as §4.6 requires.

### Examples

The basis of §11.3 consists of the eleven graphs with a rational tail `{1, i}`; for the first of them the decoration retains the flags `2, …, 12` minus `i`, together with the attaching flag.

### Dependencies

Layers 0–5 and 9–10; StableReductionPartII §§3.1–3.2; PELModuli §M5.2; SchurWeyl, Layers 5 and 7; DeligneWeightsAndPurity §8.24 (smooth proper DM duality); MixedHodgeModulesAndIrregularHodgeTheory with gap:dm-stack-mixed-hodge.

## Layer 12: Cusp homology and the realization classification in degrees thirteen to fifteen

The final induction uses the finite cusp bases and the oriented weight-fifteen complex. The passage from Hodge to Galois realizations uses the same geometric maps, not a comparison of Hodge numbers.

### 12.1 Cusp generation of degree-thirteen homology

For every stable pair prove that the STE generated by `H¹¹(M̄_{1,11})` contains `H^{2d−13}(M̄_{g,n})`, the cohomological form of `H₁₃` (CLP, Theorem 1.6, p. 2; proof, pp. 23–24). Route: genus zero has no odd cohomology. For `g ≥ 1`, §2.3 and §0.4 reduce to the pairs with `13 ≥ 2g, n ≤ 1` or `13 ≥ 2g − 2 + n, n ≥ 2`; all of them except `(2, 11), (1, 11), (1, 12), (1, 13)` have the CKgP (§§5.4, 6.9), so their pure Borel–Moore homology in degree thirteen vanishes by §5.3; `(2, 11)` follows from §11.10 and hard Lefschetz; genus one follows from §3.2 by induction on `n`. In the Künneth decomposition of the boundary term `H₁₃(D̃)` of §0.4 exactly one factor has odd homological degree, eleven or thirteen, since odd homology `H_a(M̄_v) ≅ H^a(M̄_v)^∨` vanishes for `a ≤ 9` by §9.8 and duality, and the other factor, when the stratum has two vertices, has homological degree two or zero, tautological by §10.4. A factor `H₁₃` is in `S_ω` by induction on the stable-pair order. A factor `H₁₁(M̄_v) = H^{2d_v−11}(M̄_v)(d_v)` is zero if `d_v < 11`, being dual to `H¹¹(M̄_v)`, which by §10.9 is nonzero only for `g_v = 1`, `n_v ≥ 11`, where `d_v = n_v ≥ 11`; and for `d_v ≥ 11` it is `a^{d_v−11} · H¹¹(M̄_v)` by hard Lefschetz with a tautological ample class `a` (§4.12), hence in `S_ω` by §10.9.

*Needs:* §§0.4, 2.3, 3.2, 4.4, 4.12, 5.3, 5.4, 6.9, 9.8, 10.4, 10.9, 11.10; DeligneWeightsAndPurity, gap:dm-stack-hard-lefschetz; MixedHodgeModulesAndIrregularHodgeTheory, gap:dm-stack-mixed-hodge; StableReductionPartII, gap:pointed-coarse-projectivity.

**Checks.**

- The statement concerns `H₁₃`, that is `H^{2d−13}`, not `H¹³`; for `H¹³` itself generation by `S₁₂` is the subject of CLP Remark 7.8.
- `(2, 11)` is a base case because `2g − 2 + n = 13` and genus two has no CKgP at eleven markings (`c(2) = 10`).
- No genus-seven pair is a base case: `2g = 14 > 13` for `n ≤ 1`, and `2g − 2 + n ≥ 14 > 13` for `n ≥ 2`. The largest genus is six, with `(6, n ≤ 3)` inside `c(6) = 5`, and the genus-by-genus ranges `(1, ≤ 13)`, `(2, ≤ 11)`, `(3, ≤ 9)`, `(4, ≤ 7)`, `(5, ≤ 5)`, `(6, ≤ 3)` against `c = 10, 10, 11, 11, 7, 5` leave exactly the four exceptions; the genus-seven theorem §7.7 first enters in degree fifteen (§12.2).
- Boundary factors are read in homological degree. For `M̄_{3,12}` and its divisor `M̄_{1,12} × M̄_{2,2}`, `H₁₃` of the divisor contains `H₁₁(M̄_{1,12}) ⊗ H₂(M̄_{2,2}) = H¹³(M̄_{1,12})(12) ⊗ H⁸(M̄_{2,2})(5)`, with `d = 12` and `d = 5`. The even factor `H⁸(M̄_{2,2})` is `H₂`, tautological by §10.4 at `k = 2`, and lies outside §10.1, which stops at cohomological degree three; the odd factor is `H¹³(M̄_{1,12}) = a · H¹¹(M̄_{1,12})`, in `S_ω` by hard Lefschetz and §10.9, not itself a group `H¹¹`.

### 12.2 Two-cusp generation of degree-fifteen homology

For every stable pair prove that the STE generated by `H¹¹(M̄_{1,11})` and `H¹⁵(M̄_{1,15})` contains `H^{2d−15}(M̄_{g,n})` (CLP, Theorem 1.7, p. 2; proof, p. 27). Route: as in §12.1; the base cases with `g ≥ 3` have the CKgP (§§5.4, 6.9, 7.7); the remaining ones are `g = 1`, `11 ≤ n ≤ 14` (and `n = 15`, which is a generator), by §3.2, and `g = 2`, `11 ≤ n ≤ 13`, by §11.10, §11.9 and hard Lefschetz; in the Künneth decomposition of the boundary term `H₁₅(D̃)` one factor has odd homological degree `11`, `13` or `15`, `H₁₁` treated as in §12.1, `H₁₃` by §12.1 and `H₁₅` by induction on the stable-pair order, and the other, when the stratum has two vertices, has even homological degree at most four, tautological by §10.4.

*Needs:* §§12.1, 11.9, 11.10, 11.6, 2.3, 7.7, 5.4, 6.9, 3.2, 4.12, 9.8, 10.4, 10.9; DeligneWeightsAndPurity, gap:dm-stack-hard-lefschetz; StableReductionPartII, gap:pointed-coarse-projectivity.

**Checks.**

- The degree-fifteen genus-one seed is needed at `(1, 15)`, where `W₁₅H¹⁵(M_{1,15}) = S₁₆` is not generated by `S₁₂`.
- For `g = 2`, `2g − 2 + n ≤ 15` gives `n ≤ 13`, so `(2, 13)` is the last genus-two base case.

### 12.3 Realizations in the complementary odd degrees

For every stable pair prove that `H^{2d−13}(M̄_{g,n})^ss` is a sum of copies of `L^{d−12} S₁₂`, and `H^{2d−15}(M̄_{g,n})^ss` a sum of copies of `L^{d−13} S₁₂` and `L^{d−15} S₁₆`, in both realizations, multiplicities depending on the pair (CLP, Corollary 7.2, p. 24, and Corollary 7.11, p. 28). Route: each clutching pushforward adds a twist `L` and each forgetful pullback preserves weight; follow the inductions of §§12.1–12.2, with `H¹⁵(M̄_{1,15})^ss ≅ S₁₆ ⊕ (L² S₁₂)^{⊕186263}` from §3.6 (CLP, Corollary 7.11, citing Getzler, p. 491).

*Needs:* §§12.1, 12.2, 0.5, 3.6.

**Checks.**

- The weights are `2d − 13` and `2d − 15`: `L^{d−12} S₁₂` has weight `2(d − 12) + 11 = 2d − 13`.
- These are statements about semisimplifications; equal-weight Galois extensions are not split.

### 12.4 A geometric splitting of `H¹⁵(M̄_{1,15})`

Let `V ⊂ H¹⁵(M̄_{1,15})` be the image of the boundary pushforwards and `W` the image of `H_c^{15}(M_{1,15}) → H¹⁵(M̄_{1,15})`. Prove `H¹⁵(M̄_{1,15}) = V ⊕ W` as Hodge structures and as Galois representations, with `W ≅ S₁₆` and `V^ss ≅ (L² S₁₂)^{⊕186263}`, and that `V` lies in the STE generated by `H¹¹(M̄_{1,11})` (CLP, Lemma 8.3, p. 29). Route: `0 → V → H¹⁵ → W₁₅H¹⁵(M_{1,15}) ≅ S₁₆ → 0` by §0.4; under Poincaré duality the inclusion of `W` is dual to this quotient; `V` has Hodge types `(13, 2), (2, 13)` and `W` types `(15, 0), (0, 15)`, so `V ⊥ W` and the pairing on `W` is nondegenerate; the splitting is defined by maps of varieties over `ℚ` and is therefore Galois-equivariant; `V ⊂ S_ω` by §4.8.

*Needs:* §§0.4, 0.5, 3.2, 3.6, 4.8, 12.3; MixedHodgeModulesAndIrregularHodgeTheory, gap:dm-stack-mixed-hodge; WeilConjectures, WC.1 (stack Artin comparison).

**Checks.**

- The orthogonality uses Hodge types of actual geometric subspaces; an identity of virtual classes would not split the extension.
- `dim W = 2`, and `dim V = 2·186263 = 372526`.

### 12.5 Degree fifteen in genus two

For every `n`, with `d = 3 + n`, prove that the STE generated by `H¹¹(M̄_{1,11})` contains `H^{2d−15}(M̄_{2,n})`, and that `H¹⁵(M̄_{2,n})^ss` is a sum of copies of `L² S₁₂` as Hodge structures and as Galois representations (CLP, Lemma 8.1, p. 28). Route: by §12.2 the group `H^{2d−15}(M̄_{2,n})` is a quotient of a sum of images of geometric maps from twists of `H¹¹(M̄_{1,11})` and `H¹⁵(M̄_{1,15})`; restricted to `W ≅ S₁₆` (§12.4) each such map is zero, since by §11.1 and duality the target has no Galois constituent `L^{d−15}S₁₆` and a nonzero image of the simple object would be one; restricted to `V` it lands in the STE of `H¹¹(M̄_{1,11})`. Duality gives the statement for `H¹⁵`, and the geometric maps give it as Hodge structures. This does not assert STE generation of `H¹⁵(M̄_{2,n})` for all `n`.

*Needs:* §§11.1, 12.2, 12.3, 12.4, 4.8; WeilConjectures, WC.1 (stack Artin comparison); DeligneWeightsAndPurity §8.24 (smooth proper DM duality).

**Checks.**

- Excluding a constituent after semisimplification does not by itself produce STE maps: the splitting of §12.4 is what removes the `W`-part of the exhibited maps.
- The statement for `H¹⁵(M̄_{2,n})` is CLP Conjecture 7.12 in genus two only after duality and only for `H_{15}`; `H¹⁵(M̄_{2,12})` itself is §11.9.

### 12.6 The oriented weight-fifteen boundary complex

For a stable pair `(g, n)` let `C^•_{15}(g, n)` be the weight-fifteen row of the oriented boundary complex of §10.7, so that `C⁰_{15}(g, n) = H¹⁵(M̄_{g,n})` and `C^j_{15}(g, n) = ⊕_{|E(Γ)|=j} (H¹⁵(M̄_Γ) ⊗ det ℚ^{E(Γ)})^{Aut Γ}`, with `α = d₀` and `β = d₁` (CLP, equations (8.1)–(8.2), p. 28). Prove that `α` and `β` preserve Hodge types, so that the `(15, 0)` parts form a subcomplex, and that `ker α = Gr^W_{15} H_c^{15}(M_{g,n})`. The vertex factors are compact moduli, not open strata.

*Needs:* §10.7; MixedHodgeModulesAndIrregularHodgeTheory, gap:dm-stack-mixed-hodge; §1.3.

API:

- `α` is pullback to the normalized one-edge boundary strata, and `β α = 0`.
- `α` and `β` preserve Hodge types; the `(15, 0)` parts `C^j_{15}(g, n)^{15,0}` form a subcomplex.
- `ker α = Gr^W_{15} H_c^{15}(M_{g,n})`, so `α` is injective when that group vanishes.
- `β` carries the action of graph automorphisms on `det ℚ^{E}`, and the complex is `S_n`-equivariant.

**Checks.**

- A transposition of two edges acts by `−1` on `det ℚ^E`.
- A two-edge stratum with an edge-exchanging automorphism acting trivially on an `H¹⁵` summand contributes nothing to `C²_{15}` after the orientation twist.
- The ordinary graph transform of §1.3 has no such determinant.

### 12.7 Injectivity in the weight-fifteen row

For `g ≥ 3` and every `n` prove that `α : H¹⁵(M̄_{g,n}) → C¹_{15}(g, n)` is injective (CLP, Lemma 8.2, p. 28). Route: `α` is injective when `Gr^W_{15} H_c^{15}(M_{g,n}) = 0`; by §2.3 this holds if `n ≤ 1` and `2g > 15`, or `n ≥ 2` and `2g − 2 + n > 15`; the remaining pairs with `g ≥ 3` have the CKgP (§§5.4, 6.9, 7.7), and §5.3 with the complementary-degree duality of the Conventions, as in §5.4, gives the vanishing, `Gr^W_{15} H_c^{15}(M_{g,n})` being dual to `W_{2d−15} H^{2d−15}(M_{g,n})(d)` of odd degree. The printed proof uses only the bound `2g − 2 + n > 15`, which would leave `(8, 0)` and `(8, 1)` needing a CKgP that is not available; the bound `2g > 15` for `n ≤ 1` covers them.

*Needs:* §§12.6, 2.3, 5.3, 5.4, 6.9, 7.7; WeilConjectures, gap:open-dm-stack-duality; MixedHodgeModulesAndIrregularHodgeTheory, gap:dm-stack-mixed-hodge (duality on `M_{g,n}`).

**Checks.**

- At `g = 8`, `n ≤ 1`: `2g = 16 > 15`, so `H_c^{15}(M_{8,n}) = 0` directly; no genus-eight CKgP is used.
- At `g = 7`, the pairs `(7, 0), (7, 1), (7, 2), (7, 3)` satisfy `2g − 2 + n ≤ 15` or `2g ≤ 15` and are covered by §7.7.

### 12.8 No Hodge type `(15, 0)` in genus at least two

For all `g ≥ 2` and `n` prove `H^{15,0}(M̄_{g,n}) = H^{0,15}(M̄_{g,n}) = 0` (CLP, proof of Theorem 1.1, pp. 28–29, Figure 3). Route: genus two is §12.5. For `g ≥ 3` induct on `g` and show that `β` is injective on the `(15, 0)` part of `C¹_{15}`, which with `βα = 0` and §12.7 gives the claim. A nonseparating one-edge summand has a vertex of genus `g − 1 ≥ 2`, so its `(15, 0)` part vanishes by induction. A separating summand can carry `(15, 0)` only on a genus-one side with at least fifteen flags, the other side having genus `g − 1 ≥ 2`; adding a loop at that other vertex lowers its genus to `g − 2` and gives a two-edge graph `Γ′` whose restriction map is injective on the genus-one cusp summand, while the other term of `β` on that summand comes from a source whose `(15, 0)` part vanishes.

*Needs:* §§12.5, 12.6, 12.7, 12.3.

**Checks.**

- Adding the loop preserves the total genus and lowers the vertex genus from `g − 1` to `g − 2`.
- The new vertex is stable even when `g − 2 = 1`, since it carries the separating flag and both loop flags.
- At `g = 1` the statement fails: `H^{15,0}(M̄_{1,15}) ≅ S₁₆^{15,0} ≠ 0`.

### 12.9 The realization classification

For every stable pair prove `H¹³(M̄_{g,n})^ss ≅ ⊕ L S₁₂` and `H¹⁴(M̄_{g,n})^ss ≅ ⊕ L⁷`, and for `g ≥ 2` prove `H¹⁵(M̄_{g,n})^ss ≅ ⊕ L² S₁₂`, as rational Hodge structures and as `ℚ_ℓ[G_ℚ]`-modules (CLP, Theorem 1.1, p. 1; §8, pp. 28–30). Route: duality applied to §§10.5 and 12.3 gives degrees thirteen and fourteen and leaves `S₁₆` as the only possible additional constituent in degree fifteen; §12.8 excludes it as a Hodge structure for `g ≥ 2`. For the Galois statement, the inductive maps of §12.2 exhibit `H^{2d−15}(M̄_{g,n})` as a quotient of twists of `H¹¹(M̄_{1,11})` and `H¹⁵(M̄_{1,15})`; each map restricted to `W ≅ S₁₆` (§12.4) is zero as a morphism of Hodge structures, hence of rational vector spaces, hence of Galois representations through the comparison; so no Galois constituent `S₁₆` remains. A comparison of Hodge numbers alone would not give this.

*Needs:* §§10.5, 12.3, 12.4, 12.8; MotivesAndAlgebraicCycles, gap:dm-stack-cycle-class; WeilConjectures, WC.1 (stack Artin comparison); DeligneWeightsAndPurity §8.24 (smooth proper DM duality).

**Checks.**

- At `(1, 15)` there is a summand `S₁₆`; the hypothesis `g ≥ 2` is essential.
- Each `L S₁₂` has rational dimension two and weight thirteen.
- `H¹⁴` has exponent `7`, while §10.5 has exponent `d − 7`; the two agree under duality.

### 12.10 Cohomology of genus-three moduli through eleven markings

For `n ≤ 11` prove that every `H^k(M̄_{3,n})^ss` is a finite direct sum of tensor products of powers of `L` and `S₁₂`, in both realizations; equivalently its class lies in the semiring generated by `L` and `S₁₂` (CLP, Theorem 1.9, p. 3, and its proof, p. 13). Route: by hard Lefschetz it suffices to treat `k ≤ d = 6 + n ≤ 17`. The boundary terms are products of groups `H^{k′}(M̄_{g′,n′})` with `k′ ≤ 15` and `g′ ≤ 3`: the boundary divisors of `M̄_{3,n}` have normalizations `M̄_{2,n+2}`, `M̄_{1,a} × M̄_{2,b}` with `a ≤ n + 1 ≤ 12`, and `M̄_{0,a} × M̄_{3,m}` with `a ≥ 3` and `m ≤ n − 1`. These groups are polynomials in `L` and `S₁₂`. In odd degrees this follows from §§9.8, 4.7, 10.9 and 12.9; for genus-one factors in degree fifteen it follows from §3.6, using that they have at most twelve flags; genus-zero factors have no odd cohomology (§2.6). In even degrees the groups are tautological, hence sums of copies of a power of `L` (§0.1): by §2.6 in genus zero, §3.3 in genus one and §4.3 in genus two, whose range `2r ≤ 20` contains every even `k′ ≤ 14`. A genus-three factor `M̄_{3,m}`, `m < n`, is covered in every degree by induction on `n`, starting from `n ≤ 1`, where no such factor occurs. The pure part `W_kH^k(M_{3,n})` is tautological by §§5.3–5.4.

*Needs:* §§0.1, 0.4, 2.6, 3.3, 3.6, 4.3, 4.7, 5.3, 5.4, 9.8, 10.9, 12.9; DeligneWeightsAndPurity, gap:dm-stack-hard-lefschetz; MotivesAndAlgebraicCycles, gap:dm-stack-cycle-class; StableReductionPartII, gap:pointed-coarse-projectivity.

**Checks.**

- The semiring permits products such as `S₁₂ ⊗ S₁₂`, but for `n ≤ 11` no boundary stratum produces one, and the theorem does not assert that every monomial occurs. Two genus-one vertices carrying `H¹¹` need at least `22` flags. A stable genus-three graph with exactly two genus-one vertices and `r` rational vertices has `b₁ = 1`, `r + 2` edges and at most `n + 4 − r ≤ 15` flags on the two; with three genus-one vertices it is a tree with at most `n + 3 − r ≤ 14` flags on any two of them. Outside this range, the separating genus-two graph joining two genus-one vertices with ten legs each, of type `(2, 20)`, carries `H¹¹ ⊗ H¹¹` on its normalization, in degree `11 + 11 + 2 = 24` after pushforward; nothing here asserts that this pushforward is nonzero.
- The statement is about semisimplified realizations in a Grothendieck semiring, not a decomposition of Chow motives.
- The even-degree inputs are needed: at `n = 11` and `k = 8` the loop divisor contributes `H⁶(M̄_{2,13})`, which is in no odd-degree input and above the degrees `≤ 3` of §10.1; it is `RH⁶(M̄_{2,13})` by §4.3 with `r = 3 ≤ 10`, a sum of copies of `L³`. Likewise at `n = 6` and `k = 8` the divisor `M̄_{0,3} × M̄_{3,5}` contributes `H⁶(M̄_{3,5})`, of dimension `d = 11`; it lies outside §§10.1–10.3 (degree six, genus below ten) and outside §10.4 (`2d − 6 = 16 > 14`), and is the case `n = 5` of the induction.
- For `n ≤ 11` the genus-one factors of the boundary divisors of `M̄_{3,n}` have at most `n + 1 ≤ 12` flags, and by §3.6 their odd cohomology of degree at most fifteen is a sum of copies of `L^j S₁₂`, `S₁₆` first occurring with fifteen flags; genus-two factors `M̄_{2,n+2}` are covered for every number of markings by §§11.1 and 12.9.

### Examples

`H¹³(M̄_{1,12}) ≅ (L S₁₂)^{⊕11}`; `H¹⁵(M̄_{2,12}) ≅ (L² S₁₂)^{⊕836}`; `H¹⁵(M̄_{1,15}) ≅ S₁₆ ⊕ V` with `V^ss ≅ (L² S₁₂)^{⊕186263}`.

### Dependencies

Layers 0–11; DeligneWeightsAndPurity §8.24 with gap:dm-stack-hard-lefschetz; MixedHodgeModulesAndIrregularHodgeTheory with gap:dm-stack-mixed-hodge; MotivesAndAlgebraicCycles with gap:dm-stack-cycle-class; WeilConjectures, WC.1; StableReductionPartII §§3.1–3.2, with gap:pointed-coarse-projectivity.

## Corrections to the sources

The statements of this roadmap correct the following printed passages. Each correction is used where indicated; none changes a main theorem of the source.

- CLP, Lemma 2.1, hypothesis (2.2), pp. 4–5: only smaller triples with `k′ ≤ k − 2` are required, although the proof uses the same-degree triple with one marking fewer. The corrected criterion is §4.5, with the counterexample `S = RH^*`, `(g, n, k) = (1, 12, 11)` to the printed form. CLP Theorem 1.3 remains valid, its seed region containing the same-degree triples; for the application to Corollary 2.7 see the next entry.
- CLP, sentence before Corollary 2.7, p. 7: the corollary is deduced by applying Lemma 2.1 to `RH^*`. The hypothesis of the lemma fails there at the triple `(1, 11, 11)`, where the quotient is `S₁₂` and `RH¹¹ = 0`: in the printed form for instance at `(2, 20)` in degree fourteen (`11 ≤ 14 − 2`), and in the corrected form of §4.5 already in degree twelve. The corollary holds; §4.3 proves it from Petersen's theorem for `n < 20` and, for `n ≥ 20`, from the boundary sequence and the vanishing of odd cohomology through degree nine, which leave only even Künneth factors.
- CLP, Lemma 3.2, p. 10: the proof asserts that every boundary class of `H^k(M̄_{2,n})` lies in the extension generated by the three listed families, although a boundary class whose genus-two factor has fewer than `n − 1` markings, or whose genus-one factor has degree below `k − 2`, is not a generator. §4.6 closes this through forgetful pullback to `(2, n − 1)` with the projection formula and through re-association of the expanded graphs, which works whenever at most one vertex carries a non-tautological class. The remaining boundary classes span the two-cusp space `T^k_n` of separating strata with two odd genus-one factors, which vanishes for odd `k` and for `k ≤ 23`. For even `k ≥ 24` the proof gives no argument that `T^k_n` lies in the extension generated by the printed families; §4.6 lists `T^k_n` among the generators for every `k`, and the printed form in these degrees is neither asserted nor refuted here (§4.6, fourth Check, records why a formal calculation on boundary tensors does not decide it). CLP's own applications (§7, `k = 13` and `k = 15`) have `T^k_n = 0`.
- CLP, proof of Proposition 2.2, pp. 5–6: the lowest-weight part of `H¹(M_{1,1}, V^{⊗(k−1)})` is identified with `S_{k+1}`; it also contains the twisted cusp realizations `L^{(k−1−j)/2} S_{j+2}` of the summands of `V^{⊗(k−1)}` produced by insertions of the symplectic class, for instance `(L S₁₂)^{⊕11}` at `k = 13`, where `S₁₄ = 0`. These summands vanish on `M_{1,n}`, as the diagonal classes do, and the proposition is unaffected (§3.2).
- CLP-H11, arXiv version 2, Corollary 2.5, p. 7: injectivity of the pullback from `H^{11,0}(M̄_{1,n})` to the divisors `D_B`, `|B| = 10`, is printed for every `n`; it needs `n ≥ 12`, since at `n = 11` no such divisor exists and the restriction of the rank-two group is zero (§4.7). The genus-two argument of CLP-H11 §4.3 applies it only to factors with at least twelve flags, and treats a separating stratum with two markings or more on each side through the opposite factor (§10.8), so CLP-H11 Theorem 1.1 is unaffected.
- Canning–Larson genus seven, Remark 2.3, pp. 6–7: the Hurwitz stack is called the quotient by `PGL₂` of a parametrized Hurwitz scheme. The stack of covers of a fixed line is not a scheme where a cover has an automorphism over `ℙ¹`, for instance at a bielliptic genus-seven curve, which has gonality four; §6.3 presents the `SL₂`-lift instead as a quotient of the scheme of framed covers, on which gap:equivariant-grr is applied. The Chow computations of the source are unaffected.
- CLP, §5.3.1, setup before Lemma 5.8, p. 18: the pulled-back quadratic bundle is written `U ⊕ U^∨`; `P`-equivariantly it is an extension `0 → U → V → U^∨ → 0` (§7.1). The Chern identities used are unaffected.
- CLP, proof of Lemma 5.10, p. 19: a trivialization of the underlying vector bundle is used as a trivialization of the quadratic form; §7.2 routes the argument through the parabolic reduction.
- CLP, §5.3, equations (5.10)–(5.13), pp. 20–22: the half-spin representation is treated as a representation of `SO₁₀` and the quotient by `SO₁₀` is identified with `M°_7`. The half-spin representation is a representation of `Spin₁₀`, on which `−1` acts by `−1`, and the effective quotient is by `PSO₁₀`; §§7.3–7.4 use the `Spin₁₀`-lift, the rigidification by `μ₄` and a separate rational Chow transfer. For `n = 0` the fibre-power argument of Lemma 5.13 is replaced by the forgetful route of §7.7.
- CLP, §6.2, first sentence of the proof of Theorem 1.5(3), p. 23: the range is written `k ≥ 14`; the theorem and the argument use `k ≤ 14` (§10.4).
- CLP, proof of Lemma 7.3, p. 27: the representation spanned by the symbols `M_{ij}` is written as induced from `S₁₀`; it is induced from `S₁₀ × S₂`, of dimension `66`, with the printed decomposition `T_{(2,1^{10})} ⊕ T_{(3,1^9)}` (§11.8).
- CLP, proof of Lemma 8.2, p. 28: only the bound `2g − 2 + n > 15` is used; for `n ≤ 1` the bound `2g > 15` is needed to cover `(8, 0)` and `(8, 1)` (§12.7).
- CLP, Introduction, p. 3: unimodality of the dimensions of an STE in each parity is asserted; hard Lefschetz gives injectivity up to the middle (§4.12), and decrease above the middle is not part of this roadmap's statement.
- BFP, Corollary 3.2, p. 1330: the conclusion names an arbitrary `M̄_{g,n}`; it concerns `M̄_{4,n}`, and all genera follow through the induction of §2.5 (§§9.3, 9.8).
- BFP, proof of Proposition 3.1, p. 1330: the error term in the trace comparison is written with exponent `d/2`; the hypothesis gives exponent `s/2` (WeilConjectures, WC.5, local cutoff, uses `s`).
- BFP, §10.1, p. 1355: in the displayed count of `M_{4,3}` the coefficient `−11` is attached to `q⁴`; it belongs to `q³` (§9.6).
- BFP, Remark 2.2, p. 1328: Proposition 2.1 is cited as Proposition 2.
- BFP, opening of §9, p. 1351: the Getzler–Kapranov formula is cited as Theorem 8.3; it is Theorem 8.13 (§1.4).
- BFP, equation (3), p. 1331: the `E₁` terms of the weight spectral sequence of normalized closed strata omit the orientation character `det ℚ^{E(G)}`; with it the graph invariants change, as the two-edge stratum of `M̄_{1,2}` shows (§1.5; DeligneWeightsAndPurity §8.24 retains the character).
- BFP, §9.1, p. 1351, last sentence on twisted counts: for a nonproper variety the trace formula uses compactly supported cohomology; for `𝔸¹` ordinary cohomology gives trace `1` while the count is `q`.
- BFP, Definition 9.1, p. 1351: an arbitrary twisted count is assigned an element of the representation ring; in general it is a class function, and lattice membership needs the hypotheses of WeilConjectures, WC.1 (§1.5, second Check).
- Getzler, semi-classical, final table, row `n = 14`, p. 491 (arXiv p. 10): the `S₁₂` coefficient of `e(M̄_{1,14})` is printed as `−(286L³ + 6006L² + 286L)`; it is `−(286L³ + 6006L² + 6006L + 286)`, symmetric under Poincaré duality on `M̄_{1,14}` and with degree-eleven coefficient `binom(13, 10) = 286` (§3.6, third Check). The multiplicity `6006` of `L S₁₂` in `H¹³(M̄_{1,14})`, which CLP Lemma 7.6 takes from this table, is unaffected.
- Gorsky, arXiv version 3, Theorem 3.8, p. 6, and Definition 3.5 with Lemma 3.6, p. 5: the genus of the quotient is printed as `½(1 − Σ_{j=1}^{r} k_j)` and the modulus in the local congruence count as `k`; the genus is `1 − ½ Σ_{j=1}^{r} k_j` and the modulus is `r`, as in §9.1 (the misplaced `½` is noted in Kannan, Remark 3.8, pp. 11–12).
- Kannan, arXiv version 2, Remark 3.8, pp. 11–12: the corrected quotient genus is printed with the sum running to `r − 1`; it runs to `r`, the identity automorphism in genus four giving `h = 4` only then (§9.1).

## Downstream consumers

Its natural consumers are roadmaps on the motivic structures of moduli spaces (the Chenevier–Lannes classification of motives of small conductor in the cohomology of `M̄_{g,n}`), on point counts of moduli stacks, and on tautological rings in higher degree; they would consume the classification of §12.9, the finite generation of §4.9 and the counts of Layer 9.
